%%% ----------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Rotors Between Tangent Planes}
\label{section:ga_rotor}

\subsection{The smallest rotor taking one normal to another}

\begin{prop}
\label{prop:ga_rotor}
For unit vectors $a$ and $c$ with $a \cdot c \neq -1$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eqn:ga_rotor}
Q = \frac{1 + c\, a}{|1 + c\, a|} = \frac{1 + c\, a}{\sqrt{2 (1 + a \cdot c)}}
\end{equation}
is the rotor that turns $a$ to $c$ in the plane $a \wedge c$, by their angle; it is the rotation of the Rodrigues formula~(\ref{eqn:rodrigues}). It takes the bivector $I a$ to $I c$.
\end{prop}
\begin{proof}
$c a = c \cdot a + c \wedge a$ is the product of two unit vectors, a rotor up to normalization that turns by twice their angle $\phi$. The half-way vector $m = (a + c) / |a + c|$ splits that angle in two, and $c m$, the product of two unit vectors at the angle $\phi/2$, is the rotor that turns by $\phi$ from $a$ through $m$ to $c$: $c m\, a\, m c = c (m a m) c = c\, c\, c = c$, because reflecting $a$ in the line of $m$ gives $c$. Now $c m = c (a + c) / |a + c| = (1 + c a) / |a + c|$, and $|a + c|^2 = 2 (1 + a \cdot c)$. The plane of the rotation is that of $c \wedge a$, so its axis is $a \times c$, as in~(\ref{eqn:rodrigues}). Finally a rotor commutes with $I$, so $Q (I a) \widetilde Q = I (Q a \widetilde Q) = I c$.
\end{proof}
So the rotor
\begin{equation}
\label{eqn:ga_Q}
Q_{i \to f} = \frac{1 + n_f\, n_i}{|1 + n_f\, n_i|}
\end{equation}
takes the normal of vertex $i$ to that of face $f$ and, by the last statement, the tangent bivector $\mathsf T_i = I n_i$ to $\mathsf T_f = I n_f$: it carries the tangent plane of the vertex onto that of the face, and with it every tangent vector, $a \mapsto Q_{i \to f}\, a\, \widetilde Q_{i \to f}$. The failure of \code{alignVectors} for opposite normals is visible here: $1 + n_f n_i = 0$ when $n_f = -n_i$, and no plane of rotation is singled out.

\begin{example}
\label{ex:ga_transport}
For the vertex and the face of Example~\ref{ex:transport}, $n_f n_i = n_f \cdot n_i + n_f \wedge n_i = \frac{1}{\sqrt 2} + I (n_f \times n_i)$ with $n_f \times n_i = (-\frac{1}{\sqrt 2}, 0, 0)$. Normalizing $1 + n_f n_i$, whose norm is $\sqrt{2 (1 + 1/\sqrt 2)} = 1.8478$,
\begin{equation*}
Q = 0.9239 - 0.3827\, I e_1 = \cos 22.5^\circ - I e_1 \sin 22.5^\circ = e^{-I e_1 \pi / 8} ,
\end{equation*}
the rotor of the rotation by $45^\circ$ in the plane $I e_1 = e_2 e_3$, about the $x$ axis, as found with matrices (the program prints $Q = 0.923880 + I(-0.382683, 0, 0)$). It takes $t_i = e_2$ to $c = Q e_2 \widetilde Q = (e_2 + e_3)/\sqrt 2$, and the connection is the geometric product $t_f c = e_1 (e_2 + e_3)/\sqrt 2 = (e_1 e_2 + e_1 e_3)/\sqrt 2$, a unit bivector with no scalar part. Since $\mathsf T_f = I n_f = (-I e_2 + I e_3)/\sqrt 2 = (-e_3 e_1 + e_1 e_2)/\sqrt 2 = (e_1 e_3 + e_1 e_2)/\sqrt 2$, this is $t_f c = \mathsf T_f = e^{\mathsf T_f \pi/2}$: $\rho_i^f = 90^\circ$, the same number, now read off a single product.
\end{example}

\subsection{The connection angle}

The transported first frame vector $Q t_i \widetilde Q$ and the face frame vector $t_f$ are two unit vectors of the face plane. Their geometric product is a complex number of that plane:
\begin{equation}
\label{eqn:ga_connection}
e^{\mathsf T_f \rho_i^f} = t_f\, \big( Q_{i \to f}\, t_i\, \widetilde Q_{i \to f} \big),
\end{equation}
since for unit vectors $t_f$ and $c$ in the plane, $t_f c = t_f \cdot c + t_f \wedge c = \cos \rho + \mathsf T_f \sin \rho$, with $\rho$ the angle from $t_f$ to $c$ (the bivector $t_f \wedge c$ is $\sin\rho$ times $t_f \wedge b_f = \mathsf T_f$). This single number is the discrete connection: transporting $U = t_i \psi$ from vertex $i$ into face $f$ gives
\begin{equation}
\label{eqn:ga_transport}
Q\, t_i \psi\, \widetilde Q = (Q t_i \widetilde Q)(Q \psi \widetilde Q) = t_f\, e^{\mathsf T_f \rho_i^f}\, \psi_f ,
\end{equation}
where $\psi_f = u_1 + u_2 \mathsf T_f$ is the same pair of numbers read in the face plane (because $Q \mathsf T_i \widetilde Q = \mathsf T_f$). In spinor coordinates, transport from the vertex to the face is \emph{multiplication by the unit complex number} $e^{\mathsf T_f \rho_i^f}$, and the $2 \times 2$ matrix $R_i^f$ of~(\ref{eqn:connection}) is the matrix of that multiplication in the basis $(1, \mathsf T_f)$:
\begin{equation}
\label{eqn:ga_matrix}
R_i^f = \begin{pmatrix} \cos\rho_i^f & -\sin\rho_i^f \\ \sin\rho_i^f & \cos\rho_i^f \end{pmatrix}.
\end{equation}
The transport between two vertices through the face, $(R_i^f)^T R_j^f$, is multiplication by $e^{\mathsf T (\rho_j^f - \rho_i^f)}$: the connection angles subtract, and the arbitrary angle of the face frame cancels, as found in Section~\ref{section:transport}.

%%% ----------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Intrinsic and Extrinsic Geometry}
\label{section:ga_intrinsic}

\subsection{What an inhabitant of the surface can measure}

A two-dimensional being living on the surface can measure lengths of curves and angles between them, and nothing else: it cannot see the normal, nor how the surface bends in space. Everything that can be computed from lengths and angles alone is \emph{intrinsic}; it is unchanged if the surface is bent without stretching, as a sheet of paper rolled into a cylinder. Everything else, the normal $n$, the tangent bivector $\mathsf T$ as an element of $\Ge_3$, the bending of the surface, is \emph{extrinsic}. Gauss's \emph{Theorema Egregium} says that the Gaussian curvature $K$, defined extrinsically as the product of the principal curvatures, is intrinsic.

The questions this method answers are intrinsic: parallel transport, the covariant derivative, the Dirichlet energy, the connection Laplacian, and hence the heat flow and the transported field, depend only on the metric. A paper cylinder rolled from a flat sheet transports vectors as the flat sheet does, and the method should give the same field on both. The computation, however, uses extrinsic tools: normals, rotations of $\R^3$, projections. The rest of this section separates the two.

\subsection{The Gauss formula}

Let $a$ be a tangent direction and $b$ a tangent field, and differentiate $b$ in $\R^3$: $a \cdot \nabla b$. Split the result into its tangent and normal parts with~(\ref{eqn:ga_projection}):
\begin{equation}
\label{eqn:ga_gauss}
a \cdot \nabla b = \underbrace{P(a \cdot \nabla b)}_{\nabla_a b} + \underbrace{\big((a \cdot \nabla b) \cdot n\big)\, n}_{\mathrm{II}(a, b)\, n} .
\end{equation}
The tangent part is the covariant derivative~(\ref{eqn:covariant}); the normal part is the \emph{second fundamental form}, $\mathrm{II}(a, b) = (a \cdot \nabla b) \cdot n = -b \cdot (a \cdot \nabla n)$ (differentiate $b \cdot n = 0$), which measures how the surface bends under $b$: it is extrinsic. The covariant derivative is the remaining, intrinsic part. That it depends only on the metric is the content of the fundamental theorem of Riemannian geometry: there is exactly one connection on tangent fields that is compatible with the metric, (\ref{eqn:metric_compatible}), and torsion free, $\nabla_a b - \nabla_b a = [a, b]$; it is given by the metric alone (the Koszul formula), and the projection $\nabla_a b = P(a \cdot \nabla b)$ has both properties \cite{doCarmo1976}.

\subsection{Parallel transport is rolling without twisting}

How does a tangent plane move along a curve $\gamma(s)$ if it carries its vectors parallel? The plane must follow the surface, turning $n(s)$ along with it, but it should not spin about the normal. In geometric algebra this is a rotor equation.
\begin{prop}
\label{prop:ga_rolling}
Let $R(s)$ solve $R' = \frac12 \Omega\, R$ with $R(0) = 1$, where $\Omega = n' \wedge n$ and $n(s)$ is the normal along the curve. Then $R n(0) \widetilde R = n(s)$, and for every tangent vector $a_0$ at $\gamma(0)$, the field $a(s) = R\, a_0 \widetilde R$ is tangent and parallel along the curve.
\end{prop}
\begin{proof}
For $v(s) = R v_0 \widetilde R$, the product rule and $\widetilde R' = -\frac12 \widetilde R\, \Omega$ (because $\Omega$ is a bivector, $\widetilde\Omega = -\Omega$) give $v' = \frac12 (\Omega v - v \Omega) = \Omega \cdot v$, the inner product of a bivector and a vector. With $\Omega = n' \wedge n$ and the rule $(x \wedge y) \cdot v = x (y \cdot v) - y (x \cdot v)$,
\begin{equation*}
\Omega \cdot v = n' (n \cdot v) - n (n' \cdot v).
\end{equation*}
First, $m(s) = R n(0) \widetilde R$ satisfies $m' = n' (n \cdot m) - n (n' \cdot m)$, and $m = n$ is a solution, since $n \cdot n = 1$ and $n' \cdot n = 0$; by uniqueness $m = n$. So $R$ takes the normal along the curve, hence the tangent planes onto each other, and $a(s)$ stays tangent. Second, for the tangent field $a$, $n \cdot a = 0$, so $a' = -(n' \cdot a)\, n$: the derivative of $a$ is purely normal, its tangent part vanishes, and $\nabla_{\gamma'} a = P(a') = 0$.
\end{proof}
Figure~\ref{fig:rolling} shows the rolling planes along a curve on a sphere. The bivector $\Omega = n' \wedge n$ contains the normal, so it never rotates within the tangent plane: the plane \emph{rolls} along the curve, as a coin rolls on a table, without twisting about its normal. Parallel transport is this rolling. The discrete transport $Q_{i \to f}$ of~(\ref{eqn:ga_Q}) is the same idea over a finite step: the rotor in the plane $n_f \wedge n_i$, which contains both normals and therefore has no component of spin about either of them.
\begin{figure}[t]
\centering
\adjustbox{max width=\linewidth}{\input{figures/fig_rolling}}
\caption{Parallel transport is rolling without twisting. Along a curve $\gamma$ on a sphere, the tangent plane (blue) is rolled by the rotor of Proposition~\ref{prop:ga_rolling}, here integrated as a sequence of smallest rotations between consecutive normals (red). The transported vector (green) changes only in the direction of the normal, so its covariant derivative vanishes.}
\label{fig:rolling}
\end{figure}


\subsection{What is intrinsic in the discrete transport}

On a triangle mesh the intrinsic geometry is that of the flat faces glued along their edges: it is given by the edge lengths. Transport \emph{across an edge}, from one face to the next, is intrinsic: by Proposition~\ref{prop:ga_rotor} with the two face normals, the rotor $Q$ turns about the shared edge (the direction of $n_f \times n_g$), which is exactly the unfolding of one face onto the plane of the other, an isometry that depends only on the two triangles and not on the angle at which they meet in space.

A vertex is different. It has no tangent plane: it is the apex of a cone whose total angle $\Theta_i = \sum_f \alpha_{i,f}$, the sum of the angles of the incident faces at the vertex, is in general not $2\pi$. The code gives the vertex a plane anyway, the plane orthogonal to the vertex normal~(\ref{eqn:vertex_normal}), and transports between that plane and the faces with the rolling rotor $Q_{i \to f}$. This is an \emph{extrinsic} choice: bending the mesh at the vertex without stretching any triangle changes the vertex normal and the rotors, although no length changes. The error it introduces vanishes as the mesh is refined on a smooth surface, because the vertex normal then converges to the normal of the smooth surface and the rolling rotors to its parallel transport. An intrinsic alternative \cite{Knoppel2013, Sharp2019} defines the tangent space of the vertex by the angles around it, rescaled by $2\pi / \Theta_i$ so that they close up, and the transport to a face, or across an edge, by those rescaled angles; in the code it replaces only the computation of the connection angle $\rho_i^f$ (\code{Rvf}), leaving the finite element machinery untouched. Part~\ref{part:intrinsic} develops it.

\subsection{Holonomy and curvature}

Carry a vector parallel around a small closed loop and it comes back rotated, in the tangent plane, by an angle: the \emph{holonomy} of the loop. For rolling, Proposition~\ref{prop:ga_rolling}, the angle has a geometric meaning: it is the area enclosed by the loop that the normal $n(s)$ traces on the unit sphere. By the Gauss--Bonnet theorem that area is $\int K\, dA$ over the region enclosed by the loop on the surface, which is why holonomy measures curvature and is intrinsic. On a mesh, the intrinsic transport around a vertex, unfolding the faces one after another, closes with the rotation $2\pi - \Theta_i$, the \emph{angle defect}, the discrete total curvature of the vertex. The code's transport, which rolls through the vertex normals, closes with the area of the spherical polygon whose corners are the normals visited by the loop, those of the neighboring vertices and faces, which approximates the angle defect on fine meshes; the remark of Section~\ref{section:transport} should be read in this sense.

%%% ----------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{The Covariant Derivative as a Connection on Spinor Coordinates}
\label{section:ga_gauge}

\subsection{The connection form}

Choose a frame field, a unit tangent vector $t(x)$ at each point, and write fields as $U = t \psi$ with spinor coordinates $\psi = u_1 + u_2 \mathsf T$. Differentiating along a tangent direction $a$, $a \cdot \nabla U = (a \cdot \nabla t)\, \psi + t\, (a \cdot \nabla \psi)$. The covariant derivative of the frame vector is tangent and orthogonal to $t$ (since $|t| = 1$), so it is a multiple of $t \mathsf T$:
\begin{equation}
\label{eqn:ga_omega}
\nabla_a t = \omega(a)\, t\, \mathsf T ,
\end{equation}
where the scalar $\omega(a)$, linear in $a$, is the rate at which the frame turns, measured against parallel transport: the \emph{connection form} of the frame, the angle $\theta(a)$ of~(\ref{eqn:covariant_frame}). Writing $U = u_1 t + u_2 b$ with $b = t \mathsf T$, and using also $\nabla_a b = -\omega(a)\, t$ (the frame turns rigidly, since $t \cdot b = 0$), the covariant derivative of $U$ is
\begin{equation}
\label{eqn:ga_covariant}
\nabla_a (t \psi) = t\, \big( a \cdot \nabla \psi + \omega(a)\, \mathsf T\, \psi \big) = t\, D_a \psi, \qquad D_a = a \cdot \nabla + \omega(a)\, \mathsf T ,
\end{equation}
where $a \cdot \nabla \psi = (a \cdot \nabla u_1) + (a \cdot \nabla u_2)\, \mathsf T$ differentiates the two coefficients, and $\omega \mathsf T \psi = \omega (u_1 \mathsf T - u_2)$ collects the terms $u_1 \nabla_a t + u_2 \nabla_a b$.
On spinor coordinates, the Levi-Civita connection is the ordinary derivative plus a multiplication by $\omega \mathsf T$: an infinitesimal rotation. In the language of physics, $\psi$ is a charged field and $\omega \mathsf T$ a $U(1)$ gauge potential; the connection Laplacian is the corresponding \emph{magnetic} Laplacian, and the curvature of the connection, the ``magnetic field'', is $d\omega$, which with the orientation $b = t\mathsf T = n \times t$ used here is minus the Gaussian curvature times the area form, $d\omega = -K\, dA$ \cite{Knoppel2013} (Remark~\ref{rem:structure}).

\subsection{Frame independence}

The frame $t$ is arbitrary, and nothing physical may depend on it. Rotate the frame by a field of angles, $t \to t' = t\, e^{\mathsf T \theta}$. Three things change, and we compute each.

\emph{The coordinates.} The same vector is $U = t\psi = t' \psi'$. Since $t' = t\, e^{\mathsf T\theta}$, $t\psi = t\, e^{\mathsf T\theta}\psi'$, so $\psi = e^{\mathsf T\theta}\psi'$ and $\psi' = e^{-\mathsf T \theta} \psi$.

\emph{The connection form.} By the product rule, and because $a\cdot\nabla e^{\mathsf T\theta} = (a\cdot\nabla\theta)\, \mathsf T\, e^{\mathsf T\theta}$ (the derivative of $\cos\theta + \mathsf T\sin\theta$),
\begin{equation*}
\nabla_a t' = (\nabla_a t)\, e^{\mathsf T\theta} + t\, (a \cdot \nabla\theta)\, \mathsf T e^{\mathsf T\theta} = \omega(a)\, t\mathsf T e^{\mathsf T\theta} + (a\cdot\nabla\theta)\, t\, \mathsf T e^{\mathsf T\theta} = \big(\omega(a) + a\cdot\nabla\theta\big)\; t'\mathsf T ,
\end{equation*}
using~(\ref{eqn:ga_omega}) for $\nabla_a t$, and $t\,\mathsf T e^{\mathsf T\theta} = t\, e^{\mathsf T\theta}\mathsf T = t'\mathsf T$ (even elements of the plane commute). Comparing with the definition $\nabla_a t' = \omega'(a)\, t'\mathsf T$, the new connection form is $\omega' = \omega + d\theta$.

\emph{The covariant derivative.} With the new frame,
\begin{equation*}
D'_a \psi' = a\cdot\nabla\big(e^{-\mathsf T\theta}\psi\big) + \omega'(a)\, \mathsf T\, e^{-\mathsf T\theta}\psi = -(a\cdot\nabla\theta)\,\mathsf T e^{-\mathsf T\theta}\psi + e^{-\mathsf T\theta}\, a\cdot\nabla\psi + \big(\omega(a) + a\cdot\nabla\theta\big)\,\mathsf T e^{-\mathsf T\theta}\psi .
\end{equation*}
The first and the last terms in $a\cdot\nabla\theta$ cancel, and what remains is $e^{-\mathsf T\theta}\big(a\cdot\nabla\psi + \omega(a)\mathsf T\psi\big)$:
\begin{equation}
\label{eqn:ga_gauge}
D'_a \psi' = e^{-\mathsf T \theta}\, D_a \psi .
\end{equation}
The covariant derivative transforms exactly like the coordinates themselves. So $\nabla U = t\, D\psi = t' D'\psi'$ is the same tangent object in both frames, $|D\psi|$ is frame independent, and so are the Dirichlet energy, the connection Laplacian and the solution of the heat equation. In the discrete setting this is the cancellation of the face frame in $(R_i^f)^T R_j^f$ and the statement that changing a vertex frame $t_i$ rotates the computed $u_i$ by the opposite angle and changes nothing else: the arrow $t_i \hat\psi_i$ drawn by the code is independent of the choice of $t_i$ in \code{computeVertexTangentSpace}.

\begin{rem}[Cartan's structure equations]
\label{rem:structure}
Write $\theta^1, \theta^2$ for the \emph{coframe} of the frame $(t, b)$, the 1-forms $\theta^1(X) = t\cdot X$ and $\theta^2(X) = b \cdot X$, so that $X = \theta^1(X)\, t + \theta^2(X)\, b$ and $dA = \theta^1 \wedge \theta^2$. Then
\begin{equation*}
d\theta^1 = \omega \wedge \theta^2, \qquad d\theta^2 = -\,\omega\wedge\theta^1, \qquad d\omega = -K\, \theta^1 \wedge \theta^2 .
\end{equation*}
The first two determine $\omega$ from the coframe, hence from the metric alone, which is the Levi-Civita theorem in Cartan's form; the third says that the curvature is the exterior derivative of the connection form. On the unit sphere with $t = \partial_\vartheta$, $\theta^1 = d\vartheta$, $\theta^2 = \sin\vartheta\, d\varphi$, and the equations give $\omega = \cos\vartheta\, d\varphi$ and $d\omega = -\sin\vartheta\, d\vartheta \wedge d\varphi = -dA$, so $K = 1$. Section~\ref{section:map_cartan} proves the equations and uses them to build every element of this text, and a curved one.
\end{rem}
\begin{figure}[t]
\centering
\adjustbox{max width=\linewidth}{\input{figures/fig_gauge}}
\caption{A change of frame is a change of gauge. The same tangent vector $U$ has the spinor coordinate $\psi$ in the frame $t$ and $\psi' = e^{-\mathsf T\theta}\psi$ in the frame $t' = t\, e^{\mathsf T\theta}$; nothing geometric depends on the choice.}
\label{fig:gauge}
\end{figure}


\subsection{The energy and the equation in spinor form}

With $|U| = |\psi|$ and $|\nabla_a U| = |D_a \psi|$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eqn:ga_energy}
E = \frac12 \int_\Omega \sum_k |D_{e_k} \psi|^2\, dA, \qquad \frac{\partial \psi}{\partial t} = -D^* D\, \psi ,
\end{equation}
and the backward Euler step and its weak form, (\ref{eqn:backward_euler}) and (\ref{eqn:weak}), read
\begin{equation}
\label{eqn:ga_weak}
\int_\Omega \langle \psi\, \widetilde\chi \rangle\, dA + \Delta t \int_\Omega \sum_k \langle (D_{e_k} \psi)\, \widetilde{(D_{e_k} \chi)} \rangle\, dA = \int_\Omega \langle \psi^0\, \widetilde\chi \rangle\, dA
\end{equation}
for every test spinor $\chi$, where $\langle \psi \widetilde\chi \rangle$ is the scalar part of $\psi \widetilde\chi$: for $\psi = u_1 + u_2 \mathsf T$ and $\chi = v_1 + v_2 \mathsf T$, $\widetilde \chi = v_1 - v_2 \mathsf T$ and $\langle \psi \widetilde\chi \rangle = u_1 v_1 + u_2 v_2 = U \cdot V$. This is the real part of $\psi \bar\chi$, the familiar Hermitian inner product, with the reverse playing the role of complex conjugation.

%%% ----------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{The Finite Element Method in Geometric Algebra}
\label{section:ga_fem}

\subsection{Spinor hat functions}

The trial field~(\ref{eqn:trial}), in spinor coordinates of the face, is
\begin{equation}
\label{eqn:ga_trial}
U\big|_f = t_f\, \psi_f, \qquad \psi_f = \sum_{i \in f} \phi_i\; e^{\mathsf T_f \rho_i^f}\, \psi_i ,
\end{equation}
by~(\ref{eqn:ga_transport}): each vertex coordinate is turned by its connection angle into the face, and the three are interpolated with the hats. The face is flat and $t_f$ constant, so $\omega = 0$ on the face and $D = \nabla$, and
\begin{equation}
\label{eqn:ga_grad_trial}
D_a \psi_f = \sum_{i \in f} (a \cdot \nabla \phi_i)\, e^{\mathsf T_f \rho_i^f}\, \psi_i .
\end{equation}

\subsection{The gradients of the hat functions: the reciprocal frame}

On the face with vertices $x_1, x_2, x_3$, take the \emph{edge frame} $e_1 = x_2 - x_1$, $e_2 = x_3 - x_1$, the columns of $B$ in~(\ref{eqn:affine}), and their \emph{area bivector} $E = e_1 \wedge e_2 = 2 A_f\, \mathsf T_f$. The \emph{reciprocal frame} $e^1, e^2$ is the pair of vectors of the face plane with $e^k \cdot e_m = \delta_{km}$. In geometric algebra it is written directly:
\begin{equation}
\label{eqn:ga_reciprocal}
e^1 = e_2 \cdot E^{-1}, \qquad e^2 = -\,e_1 \cdot E^{-1}, \qquad E^{-1} = -\frac{E}{|E|^2} .
\end{equation}
Indeed $e_2 \cdot E$ lies in the plane and is orthogonal to $e_2$, and $(e_2 \cdot E) \cdot e_1 = (e_2 \cdot e_1)^2 - |e_2|^2 |e_1|^2 = -|E|^2$, so $e^1 \cdot e_1 = 1$ and $e^1 \cdot e_2 = 0$; similarly for $e^2$.

\begin{prop}
\label{prop:ga_reciprocal}
The gradients of the hat functions of the face are $\nabla \phi_2 = e^1$, $\nabla \phi_3 = e^2$ and $\nabla \phi_1 = -e^1 - e^2$; the reciprocal frame is the pair of columns of $B (B^T B)^{-1}$, \code{Binv}. Equivalently, for each vertex $a$ with the opposite edge $x_c - x_b$ (in the counterclockwise order $a, b, c$),
\begin{equation}
\label{eqn:ga_grad_edge}
\nabla \phi_a = \frac{(x_c - x_b)\, \mathsf T_f}{2 A_f} :
\end{equation}
the opposite edge, turned a quarter turn inward, divided by twice the area.
\end{prop}
\begin{proof}
The gradient $g$ of $\phi_a$ lies in the plane and is determined by its inner products with the edges, $g \cdot e_m = \partial \hat\phi_a / \partial \xi_m$ (proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:gradient}). Writing $g = \sum_k (\partial \hat\phi_a / \partial \xi_k)\, e^k$ satisfies these equations, by the defining property of the reciprocal frame, and with~(\ref{eqn:shape_gradients}) gives the three gradients. The matrix $B (B^T B)^{-1}$ has the same defining property, $B^T \big(B (B^T B)^{-1}\big) = I$, and its columns lie in the plane, so its columns are $e^1$ and $e^2$. For~(\ref{eqn:ga_grad_edge}), take $a = 2$; in the counterclockwise order $a, b, c = 2, 3, 1$ its opposite edge is $x_c - x_b = x_1 - x_3 = -e_2$. Since $E = 2 A_f \mathsf T_f$ and $\mathsf T_f^{-1} = -\mathsf T_f$, $E^{-1} = -\mathsf T_f / (2 A_f)$, and by~(\ref{eqn:ga_reciprocal}) $e^1 = e_2 \cdot E^{-1} = -e_2 \mathsf T_f / (2 A_f) = (x_1 - x_3)\, \mathsf T_f / (2 A_f)$, using that for a vector in the plane of a bivector the inner and geometric products coincide. The other vertices follow by relabeling. (On the triangle $x_1 = 0$, $x_2 = e_x$, $x_3 = e_y$ with $n = e_z$: $(x_1 - x_3)\mathsf T = n \times (-e_y) = e_x$, which is indeed $\nabla \phi_2$.)
\end{proof}
The formula~(\ref{eqn:ga_grad_edge}) gives the cotangent formula of Proposition~\ref{prop:cotangent} in three lines. For the counterclockwise order $a, b, c$, the opposite edge of $a$ is $x_c - x_b$ and that of $b$ (in the order $b, c, a$) is $x_a - x_c$, so
\begin{equation*}
A_f\, \nabla\phi_a \cdot \nabla\phi_b = A_f\, \frac{(x_c - x_b)\mathsf T_f}{2A_f} \cdot \frac{(x_a - x_c)\mathsf T_f}{2A_f} = \frac{(x_c - x_b) \cdot (x_a - x_c)}{4 A_f},
\end{equation*}
because the quarter turn preserves inner products, $(u\mathsf T)\cdot(v\mathsf T) = u \cdot v$ for vectors $u, v$ of the plane. Write $u = x_b - x_c$ and $v = x_a - x_c$ for the two edges leaving $c$. Then $(x_c - x_b)\cdot(x_a - x_c) = -\,u\cdot v = -|u||v|\cos\theta_c$, and $2A_f = |u \wedge v| = |u||v|\sin\theta_c$, so
\begin{equation*}
A_f\, \nabla\phi_a \cdot \nabla\phi_b = \frac{-|u||v|\cos\theta_c}{2\,|u||v|\sin\theta_c} = -\tfrac12\cot\theta_c .
\end{equation*} In the vector manifold calculus of Hestenes and Sobczyk \cite{HestenesSobczyk1984}, the reciprocal frame is the general rule: the vector derivative on a face is $\nabla = e^1 \partial_{\xi_1} + e^2 \partial_{\xi_2}$.

\begin{example}
For the triangle of Example~\ref{ex:triangle}, $E = e_1 \wedge e_2 = I (e_1 \times e_2) = I (0, -2, 2)$, with $|E| = 2\sqrt 2 = 2 A_f$, and $\mathsf T_f = E / |E| = I n_f$. The gradient of $\phi_2$ by~(\ref{eqn:ga_grad_edge}) is the opposite edge $x_1 - x_3 = (0, -1, -1)$, turned a quarter turn in the face, $(x_1 - x_3)\, \mathsf T_f = n_f \times (x_1 - x_3) = \frac{1}{\sqrt 2} (0, -1, 1) \times (0, -1, -1) = \frac{1}{\sqrt 2} (2, 0, 0)$, divided by $2 A_f = 2\sqrt 2$: $\nabla\phi_2 = (\frac12, 0, 0)$, as in Example~\ref{ex:triangle}, without inverting any matrix.
\end{example}

\subsection{The element matrices}

For two vertices $i, j$ of the face, take the trial field with only the vertex $j$, $\psi_f = \phi_j\, e^{\mathsf T_f\rho_j^f}\psi_j$, and the test spinor with only the vertex $i$, $\chi = \phi_i\, e^{\mathsf T_f \rho_i^f}\, \chi_i$. By~(\ref{eqn:ga_grad_trial}), $D_{e_k}\psi_f = (e_k\cdot\nabla\phi_j)\, e^{\mathsf T_f\rho_j^f}\psi_j$ and $D_{e_k}\chi = (e_k\cdot\nabla\phi_i)\, e^{\mathsf T_f\rho_i^f}\chi_i$. The reverse of a product reverses the order of its factors and the reverse of $e^{\mathsf T\rho}$ is $e^{-\mathsf T\rho}$, so $\widetilde{D_{e_k}\chi} = (e_k\cdot\nabla\phi_i)\, \widetilde{\chi_i}\, e^{-\mathsf T_f\rho_i^f}$. The stiffness integrand of~(\ref{eqn:ga_weak}) is then
\begin{align*}
\sum_k \big\langle (D_{e_k} \psi_f)\, \widetilde{(D_{e_k}\chi)} \big\rangle
&= \Big(\sum_k (e_k\cdot\nabla\phi_i)(e_k\cdot\nabla\phi_j)\Big)\, \big\langle e^{\mathsf T_f \rho_j^f} \psi_j\; \widetilde{\chi_i}\, e^{-\mathsf T_f \rho_i^f} \big\rangle \\
&= (\nabla\phi_i \cdot \nabla\phi_j)\, \big\langle e^{\mathsf T_f (\rho_j^f - \rho_i^f)}\, \psi_j\, \widetilde{\chi_i} \big\rangle .
\end{align*}
In the first line the scalars $e_k\cdot\nabla\phi$ were moved out of the product; their sum over the orthonormal basis $e_1, e_2$ is the inner product of the two gradients. In the second line $e^{-\mathsf T_f\rho_i^f}$ was moved to the front: $\psi_j$, $\widetilde{\chi_i}$ and the exponentials are all even elements of the plane of the face, complex numbers, and they commute. In complex notation the integrand is $(\nabla\phi_i\cdot\nabla\phi_j)\, \mathrm{Re}\big(\overline{\chi_i}\, e^{\mathrm i(\rho_j^f - \rho_i^f)}\, \psi_j\big)$, and $\mathrm{Re}(\overline{\chi_i}\, z\, \psi_j)$ is the real inner product of $\chi_i$ with $z\psi_j$: the entry of the matrix is the complex number $z$ multiplied by the scalar part of the element. So, as a complex matrix,
\begin{equation}
\label{eqn:ga_element}
\mathrm L^f_{ij} = A_f\, (\nabla\phi_i \cdot \nabla\phi_j)\; e^{\mathsf T (\rho_j^f - \rho_i^f)}, \qquad \mathrm M^f_{ii} = \frac{A_f}{3} :
\end{equation}
one complex number per pair of vertices instead of a $2 \times 2$ block. The global stiffness $\mathrm L$ is then an $n \times n$ \emph{Hermitian} matrix, $\mathrm L_{ji} = \overline{\mathrm L_{ij}}$ (the reverse of $e^{\mathsf T \rho}$ is $e^{-\mathsf T \rho}$), the form in which discrete connection Laplacians are usually written \cite{Knoppel2013, Sharp2019}, and the backward Euler step is the complex system
\begin{equation}
\label{eqn:ga_system}
(\mathrm M + \Delta t\, \mathrm L)\, \psi = \mathrm M\, \psi^0, \qquad \psi \in \mathbb C^n .
\end{equation}

\subsection{The code solves the real form of the complex system}

A complex number $z = \alpha + \beta \mathsf T$ acts on $\psi = u_1 + u_2 \mathsf T$ by multiplication, and in the basis $(1, \mathsf T)$ that multiplication is the real $2 \times 2$ matrix $\begin{pmatrix} \alpha & -\beta \\ \beta & \alpha \end{pmatrix}$. Replacing every complex entry of~(\ref{eqn:ga_system}) by this matrix, and every complex unknown by its two real coordinates, turns the $n \times n$ complex system into a $2n \times 2n$ real one with the same solution: and that real system is exactly the one assembled in Part~\ref{part:matrix}. The element block~(\ref{eqn:element_stiffness}) is the matrix of multiplication by~(\ref{eqn:ga_element}), $(R_i^f)^T R_j^f$ being the matrix of $e^{\mathsf T (\rho_j^f - \rho_i^f)}$ by~(\ref{eqn:ga_matrix}), and the real mass and the Dirichlet rows are the real forms of their complex counterparts. The code works with real arithmetic and the general sparse solver; the complex form halves the size of the system and shows that every block of the code has the structure ``scalar times rotation''.
\begin{figure}[t]
\centering
\adjustbox{max width=\linewidth}{\input{figures/fig_complex_block}}
\caption{Every $2 \times 2$ block of the code is a real multiple of a rotation, the matrix of multiplication by a complex number $e^{\mathsf T \delta}$ of the tangent plane; the complex form stores only that number.}
\label{fig:complex_block}
\end{figure}


%%% ----------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{The Geometric Algebra in Code}
\label{section:ga_code}

The repository contains a second program, \code{ga\_fem.cpp}, which needs no graphics and implements Part~\ref{part:ga} with the small header \code{GAConnection.h}. It is built and run from \code{src/} with
\begin{lstlisting}[language=bash, numbers=none]
c++ -std=c++17 -O2 -I<eigen> ga_fem.cpp HalfEdge/Mesh.cpp -o ga_fem
./ga_fem ../fem-basic-2d/data
\end{lstlisting}
It prints the worked examples of this text, solves the cactus of \code{FEM.cpp} twice, with the real $2 \times 2$ blocks of Part~\ref{part:matrix} and with the complex Hermitian system of Part~\ref{part:ga}, solves the sphere of Example~\ref{ex:sphere}, and writes the data of Figures~\ref{fig:cactus} and~\ref{fig:sphere}. On the cactus, the two solutions agree to $6 \cdot 10^{-16}$, the rounding error of double precision: the two parts of this text compute the same thing.

\paragraph{The algebra.} Only the grades the method needs are implemented: vectors, bivectors stored by their dual vectors ($B = I b$), and rotors $w + I b$. The product of two vectors is a rotor up to normalization, and the inner product of a vector with a bivector is the quarter turn~(\ref{eqn:ga_quarter_turn}):

Compare it line by line with \code{AssembleStiffness\-Element\-Embedded2}, \code{AssembleMatrix2}, \code{IplusMinvTimesA} and \code{PreFactor}, where each complex number is a $2 \times 2$ block:

%%% ----------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Dictionary}
\label{section:ga_dictionary}

Table~\ref{tab:dictionary} lists each object of the two parts side by side, with its nature, intrinsic or extrinsic.

\begin{table}[t]
\centering
\caption{The objects of Part~\ref{part:matrix} and of Part~\ref{part:ga}, and whether they depend only on the metric of the surface (intrinsic) or on its embedding in space (extrinsic).}
\label{tab:dictionary}
\footnotesize
\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3pt}
\begin{tabular}{p{0.24\linewidth} p{0.27\linewidth} p{0.27\linewidth} p{0.14\linewidth}}
\toprule
Object & Matrices (Part~\ref{part:matrix}) & Geometric algebra & Nature \\
\midrule
tangent plane & $T T^T = I - n n^T$ & bivector $\mathsf T = I n$ & extrinsic \\
frame & $T = [t \;\; n \times t]$ & one vector $t$, $b = t \mathsf T$ & a gauge choice \\
coordinates & $u \in \R^2$ & spinor $\psi = u_1 + u_2 \mathsf T$ & gauge dependent \\
rotation in the plane & $2 \times 2$ rotation & $e^{\mathsf T \theta}$ & intrinsic \\
plane to plane & Rodrigues $Q$ & rotor $(1 + n_f n_i)/|1 + n_f n_i|$ & extrinsic tool \\
discrete connection & $R_i^f = T_f^T Q T_i$ & $e^{\mathsf T \rho_i^f} = t_f Q t_i \widetilde Q$ & see Sec.~\ref{section:ga_intrinsic} \\
transport $j \to i$ & $(R_i^f)^T R_j^f$ & $e^{\mathsf T (\rho_j - \rho_i)}$ & gauge covariant \\
covariant derivative & $\partial u + \omega u$ & $D = \nabla + \omega \mathsf T$ & intrinsic \\
hat gradients & $B (B^T B)^{-1} \hat\nabla\hat\phi$ & reciprocal frame, $\frac{(x_c - x_b)\mathsf T}{2A}$ & intrinsic \\
stiffness block & $A (g_i \cdot g_j) R_i^T R_j$ & $A (\nabla\phi_i \cdot \nabla\phi_j) e^{\mathsf T(\rho_j - \rho_i)}$ & intrinsic up to the vertex planes \\
global system & real $2n \times 2n$ & complex Hermitian $n \times n$ & --- \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

The geometric algebra formulation removes the frames from the geometry: a tangent plane is a bivector, a direction in it is a complex number of that plane, and transport is a rotor. Frames reappear only as the gauge in which coordinates are stored, and the gauge covariance~(\ref{eqn:ga_gauge}) guarantees that they do not affect the result. What remains of the embedding is the choice of a tangent plane at each vertex, the one extrinsic ingredient of the discrete connection, and the place where the intrinsic method of Part~\ref{part:intrinsic} differs.


%%% ======================================================================
\part{The Intrinsic Method: Everything from Edge Lengths}
\label{part:intrinsic}

Part~\ref{part:ga} ended with one extrinsic ingredient left in the method: the tangent plane of a vertex, which the code takes orthogonal to the vertex normal. This part removes it. We build the whole method from the edge lengths of the mesh, and nothing else: no normal, no rotor of space, no position in $\R^3$. The finite element method itself does not change at all. The element matrix~(\ref{eqn:ga_element}) needs three things per face, an area, the gradients of the three hat functions, and the connection angle $\rho_i^f$ of each corner; we compute the first two from the lengths of the face (Section~\ref{section:int_triangle}), and the third from the angles of the faces around each vertex (Sections~\ref{section:int_vertex} and~\ref{section:int_connection}).

Nothing beyond Part~\ref{part:ga} is needed, and only little of it: the law of cosines, angles, and the unit complex numbers $e^{\mathsf T \theta}$ of a plane, which here are nothing more than rotations of the plane by the angle $\theta$ (the reader may read $\mathsf T$ as $i$). We go slowly, derive every formula, and say at each step which concept of Part~\ref{part:matrix} or Part~\ref{part:ga} it replaces. The program of this part is \code{IntrinsicConnection.h} with the function \code{intrinsicform::solve} of \code{ga\_fem.cpp}; Section~\ref{section:int_experiments} compares it with Part~\ref{part:ga} on folded sheets, on spheres and on the cactus.

%%% ----------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{What It Means to Be Intrinsic}
\label{section:int_meaning}

\subsection{A folded sheet of paper}

Take a flat sheet of paper and draw on it a field of parallel arrows, all pointing the same way. On the flat sheet, these arrows are the parallel transport of each other: the vector heat method started from one of them should return all of them. Now fold the sheet, without stretching or tearing it, along a few straight lines. The arrows are still drawn on the paper. An ant walking on the paper cannot tell that the paper was folded: it measures the same lengths and the same angles as before, and to it the arrows are still parallel. Parallel transport is defined by what the ant can measure (Section~\ref{section:ga_intrinsic}), so the correct answer on the folded sheet is the same field of arrows, drawn on the folded paper.

The method of Parts~\ref{part:matrix} and~\ref{part:ga} gives that answer for some foldings and not for others. If all the folds are parallel, as when the sheet is folded into a prism, it is exact. If the folds meet at angles, as in the Miura-ori pattern of Figure~\ref{fig:miura}, a classical fold of maps and of solar panels in which four creases meet at every vertex, its vectors deviate from the drawn ones by $7.4^\circ$ on average and by up to $21.9^\circ$ (Section~\ref{section:int_experiments}), although the paper is not stretched anywhere. The culprit is the vertex normal~(\ref{eqn:vertex_normal}). At a vertex of the Miura-ori the four faces are tilted in four different directions, and their averaged normal defines a plane that is none of them and does not exist on the paper. Part~\ref{part:ga} rolls each face into that plane, each about a different axis, and the rolled faces no longer fit together edge to edge as they do on the paper. When all the folds through a vertex are parallel, every face is rolled about the same line, the fold, and rolling is exactly unfolding: that is why the prism comes out right. The method is bent by the folding, although the paper, as far as the ant knows, is not.
\begin{figure}[t]
\centering
\adjustbox{max width=0.85\linewidth}{\input{figures/fig_miura}}
\caption{A Miura-ori fold: $24 \times 16$ parallelograms, each cut into two triangles, folded so that four creases meet at every vertex. The four angles at every vertex still add up to $2\pi$, so the folded sheet is isometric to a flat one, and parallel transport on it is the transport of the flat sheet. Arrows: the exact field, started at the center, which the method of this part reproduces to $2 \cdot 10^{-13}$ degrees. Shading: the error of the method of Part~\ref{part:ga} in each face, from $0^\circ$ (white) to $21.9^\circ$ (red).}
\label{fig:miura}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Intrinsic quantities of a mesh}

On a triangle mesh, what the ant can measure is the length of every edge, and from the lengths, the angles of every triangle. Two meshes with the same triangles, glued in the same way, and the same edge lengths are \emph{isometric}: the second may be a folded copy of the first, as in Figure~\ref{fig:miura}, and the ant cannot tell them apart. A quantity is \emph{intrinsic} if it can be computed from the edge lengths alone; it is then the same on all isometric meshes.

Let us list what the program of Parts~\ref{part:matrix} and~\ref{part:ga} reads from the mesh:
\begin{enumerate}
\item the positions $x_a$ of the vertices of each face, through the matrix $B$, to compute the area and the gradients of the hat functions (Section~\ref{section:gradients});
\item the face normal $n_f$, for the face frame and the rotor $Q_{i \to f}$;
\item the vertex normal $n_i$, for the vertex frame and the rotor $Q_{i \to f}$.
\end{enumerate}
We shall see that item 1 only looks extrinsic: the area and the gradients depend on the three edge lengths alone (Section~\ref{section:int_triangle}). Items 2 and 3 enter only through the connection angle $\rho_i^f$; we replace them by angles measured on the faces (Sections~\ref{section:int_vertex} and~\ref{section:int_connection}).

%%% ----------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{A Triangle Known Only by Its Edge Lengths}
\label{section:int_triangle}

\subsection{The metric of a face is its Gram matrix}

Recall from Section~\ref{section:gradients} the affine map $F(\xi) = x_1 + B \xi$ from the reference triangle to the face, with $B = [\,x_2 - x_1 \;\; x_3 - x_1\,] = [\,e_1 \;\; e_2\,]$, a $3 \times 2$ matrix. A small step $d\xi$ in the reference triangle is carried to the step $B\, d\xi$ on the face, whose squared length is
\begin{equation*}
|B\, d\xi|^2 = d\xi^T (B^T B)\, d\xi .
\end{equation*}
So the $2 \times 2$ matrix
\begin{equation}
\label{eqn:metric}
G_f = B^T B = \begin{pmatrix} e_1 \cdot e_1 & e_1 \cdot e_2 \\ e_1 \cdot e_2 & e_2 \cdot e_2 \end{pmatrix}
\end{equation}
is the rule that measures lengths, and therefore angles, on the face, in the coordinates $\xi$ of the reference triangle. In differential geometry such a rule is called a \emph{metric}, or the \emph{first fundamental form} of the surface in the coordinates $\xi$; in linear algebra, $G_f$ is the \emph{Gram matrix} of the two edges. The matrix $B$ itself says more: where the face is in space and how it is turned. The metric keeps only what the ant can measure.

The metric is determined by the three edge lengths. Write $\ell_{12} = |e_1|$, $\ell_{13} = |e_2|$ and $\ell_{23} = |x_3 - x_2|$. The diagonal entries are $\ell_{12}^2$ and $\ell_{13}^2$. For the off-diagonal entry, the third edge is $x_3 - x_2 = e_2 - e_1$, so
\begin{equation*}
\ell_{23}^2 = |e_2 - e_1|^2 = |e_1|^2 - 2\, e_1 \cdot e_2 + |e_2|^2 ,
\end{equation*}
and solving for $e_1 \cdot e_2$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eqn:gram}
G_f = \begin{pmatrix} \ell_{12}^2 & \frac12 (\ell_{12}^2 + \ell_{13}^2 - \ell_{23}^2) \\ \frac12 (\ell_{12}^2 + \ell_{13}^2 - \ell_{23}^2) & \ell_{13}^2 \end{pmatrix}.
\end{equation}
This is the law of cosines in disguise: $e_1 \cdot e_2 = \ell_{12} \ell_{13} \cos\alpha_1$, where $\alpha_1$ is the angle of the face at $x_1$.

\begin{prop}
\label{prop:metric_only}
The area of a face, the gradients' inner products $\nabla\phi_a \cdot \nabla\phi_b$, and therefore the element stiffness and mass matrices of the scalar parts, $A_f\, \nabla\phi_a \cdot \nabla\phi_b$ and $A_f / 3$, depend on $B$ only through $G_f$, and so only on the three edge lengths.
\end{prop}
\begin{proof}
Area: by Lagrange's identity $|e_1 \times e_2|^2 = |e_1|^2 |e_2|^2 - (e_1 \cdot e_2)^2 = \det G_f$, so $A_f = \frac12 |e_1 \times e_2| = \frac12 \sqrt{\det G_f}$. Gradients: by Proposition~\ref{prop:gradient}, $\nabla\phi_a = B\, G_f^{-1} \hat\nabla\hat\phi_a$, so
\begin{equation*}
\nabla\phi_a \cdot \nabla\phi_b = \hat\nabla\hat\phi_a^T\, G_f^{-1} B^T B\, G_f^{-1}\, \hat\nabla\hat\phi_b = \hat\nabla\hat\phi_a^T\, G_f^{-1}\, \hat\nabla\hat\phi_b ,
\end{equation*}
where the reference gradients $\hat\nabla\hat\phi_a$ of~(\ref{eqn:shape_gradients}) are fixed numbers. Finally $G_f$ is given by the lengths, (\ref{eqn:gram}).
\end{proof}
So the matrix $B$, which Part~\ref{part:matrix} needed in order to find the gradients as vectors of space, was carrying more than the method uses: the method uses only its ``shadow'' $B^T B$. This settles item 1 of the list of Section~\ref{section:int_meaning}.

\subsection{Heron's formula and the angles}

Two formulas from lengths will be used directly. The first gives the area. From the proof above and~(\ref{eqn:gram}),
\begin{equation*}
16 A_f^2 = 4 \det G_f = 4 \ell_{12}^2 \ell_{13}^2 - (\ell_{12}^2 + \ell_{13}^2 - \ell_{23}^2)^2 ,
\end{equation*}
a difference of two squares, $(2 \ell_{12}\ell_{13})^2 - (\ell_{12}^2 + \ell_{13}^2 - \ell_{23}^2)^2$. Factoring it, and then each factor again as a difference of squares,
\begin{align*}
16 A_f^2 &= \big(\ell_{23}^2 - (\ell_{12} - \ell_{13})^2\big)\,\big((\ell_{12} + \ell_{13})^2 - \ell_{23}^2\big) \\
&= (\ell_{23} - \ell_{12} + \ell_{13})(\ell_{23} + \ell_{12} - \ell_{13})(\ell_{12} + \ell_{13} - \ell_{23})(\ell_{12} + \ell_{13} + \ell_{23}).
\end{align*}
With the half perimeter $\sigma = \frac12 (\ell_{12} + \ell_{13} + \ell_{23})$, the four factors are $2(\sigma - \ell_{12})$, $2(\sigma - \ell_{13})$, $2(\sigma - \ell_{23})$ and $2\sigma$, which is \emph{Heron's formula}:
\begin{equation}
\label{eqn:heron}
A_f = \sqrt{\sigma (\sigma - \ell_{12})(\sigma - \ell_{13})(\sigma - \ell_{23})} .
\end{equation}
The second gives the angles. Dividing the off-diagonal entry of~(\ref{eqn:gram}) by $\ell_{12} \ell_{13}$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eqn:law_of_cosines}
\cos\alpha_1 = \frac{\ell_{12}^2 + \ell_{13}^2 - \ell_{23}^2}{2\, \ell_{12}\, \ell_{13}} ,
\end{equation}
and the same at the other two corners, with the lengths relabeled. As a check against Part~\ref{part:matrix}: $\sin\alpha_1 = 2 A_f / (\ell_{12} \ell_{13})$ (twice the area is base times height), so $\cot\alpha_1 = (\ell_{12}^2 + \ell_{13}^2 - \ell_{23}^2)/(4 A_f)$, and the cotangent weights of Proposition~\ref{prop:cotangent} are functions of the lengths, as they must be.

\subsection{Laying the triangle out in the plane}

It is convenient to have actual coordinates, so we draw an isometric copy of the triangle in the plane $\R^2$, its \emph{layout} (Figure~\ref{fig:layout}):
\begin{equation}
\label{eqn:layout}
\bar x_1 = (0, 0), \qquad \bar x_2 = (\ell_{12}, 0), \qquad \bar x_3 = \ell_{13}\, (\cos\alpha_1, \sin\alpha_1).
\end{equation}
It has the right lengths: $|\bar x_2 - \bar x_1| = \ell_{12}$, $|\bar x_3 - \bar x_1| = \ell_{13}$, and $|\bar x_3 - \bar x_2|^2 = \ell_{13}^2 - 2 \ell_{12} \ell_{13} \cos\alpha_1 + \ell_{12}^2 = \ell_{23}^2$ by~(\ref{eqn:law_of_cosines}). It is counterclockwise, because $\sin\alpha_1 > 0$. Its Gram matrix is therefore~(\ref{eqn:gram}), and by Proposition~\ref{prop:metric_only} any quantity of the method computed on the layout equals the one computed on the face in space.

On the layout we compute the gradients with the formula~(\ref{eqn:ga_grad_edge}) of Part~\ref{part:ga}: the opposite edge, turned a quarter turn counterclockwise, divided by twice the area. In the plane the quarter turn is $J(x, y) = (-y, x)$, so for the counterclockwise order $a, b, c$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eqn:intrinsic_gradient}
\nabla\phi_a = \frac{J (\bar x_c - \bar x_b)}{2 A_f} .
\end{equation}
The layout also gives the face its frame: the $x$ axis, which is the direction of the first edge, from $\bar x_1$ to $\bar x_2$. This is the same first frame vector $t_f$ that \code{computeFaceTangentSpace} of \code{FEM.cpp} takes. A direction in the face is now simply an angle measured from the $x$ axis; we call $\beta_e$ the angle of the direction of an edge $e$ of the face in this frame (the code's \code{edgeAngle}).
\begin{figure}[t]
\centering
\adjustbox{max width=0.42\linewidth}{\input{figures/fig_layout_3d}}\hfill
\adjustbox{max width=0.52\linewidth}{\input{figures/fig_layout_2d}}
\caption{Left: the triangle of Example~\ref{ex:triangle} in space; Part~\ref{part:intrinsic} reads only its three edge lengths. Right: its layout~(\ref{eqn:layout}), an isometric copy in the plane, with the face frame $t_f$ along the first edge. Every quantity of the method can be computed on the layout.}
\label{fig:layout}
\end{figure}

\begin{example}
\label{ex:int_triangle}
The triangle of Example~\ref{ex:triangle}, $x_1 = 0$, $x_2 = (2, 0, 0)$, $x_3 = (0, 1, 1)$, has $\ell_{12} = 2$, $\ell_{13} = \sqrt 2$ and $\ell_{23} = |(-2, 1, 1)| = \sqrt 6$. By~(\ref{eqn:gram}), $G_f = \begin{pmatrix} 4 & 0 \\ 0 & 2 \end{pmatrix}$, exactly the $B^T B$ of Example~\ref{ex:triangle}, now obtained without the positions. Heron's formula with $\sigma = (2 + \sqrt 2 + \sqrt 6)/2$ gives $A_f = 1.414214 = \sqrt 2$, and~(\ref{eqn:law_of_cosines}) gives the angles $\alpha_1 = 90^\circ$, $\alpha_2 = 35.26^\circ$, $\alpha_3 = 54.74^\circ$. The layout is $\bar x_1 = (0, 0)$, $\bar x_2 = (2, 0)$, $\bar x_3 = (0, \sqrt 2)$, and~(\ref{eqn:intrinsic_gradient}) gives
\begin{equation*}
\nabla\phi_1 = (-\tfrac12, -\tfrac{1}{\sqrt 2}), \qquad \nabla\phi_2 = (\tfrac12, 0), \qquad \nabla\phi_3 = (0, \tfrac{1}{\sqrt 2}),
\end{equation*}
with squared lengths $\frac34$, $\frac14$, $\frac12$: those of the gradients $(-\frac12, -\frac12, -\frac12)$, $(\frac12, 0, 0)$, $(0, \frac12, \frac12)$ of Example~\ref{ex:triangle}, which are the same vectors seen in a different plane. The stiffness $A_f\, \nabla\phi_a \cdot \nabla\phi_b$ is the matrix of Example~\ref{ex:triangle}, entry for entry (the program prints both).
\end{example}

%%% ----------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{The Tangent Plane of a Vertex, from Angles}
\label{section:int_vertex}

\subsection{The cone around a vertex}

Inside a face, the tangent plane is the plane of the face, and the layout describes it intrinsically. Across an edge, from one face to its neighbor, parallel transport is the unfolding of one face onto the plane of the other (Section~\ref{section:ga_intrinsic}), and the unfolding depends only on the two triangles. So faces and edges pose no problem. The problem is the vertex.

Cut the faces around a vertex $i$ along one edge and flatten them one after another into the plane, as in Figure~\ref{fig:cone_rescale}. Their angles at $i$ add up to the \emph{total angle}
\begin{equation}
\label{eqn:total_angle}
\Theta_i = \sum_{f \ni i} \alpha_{i,f} ,
\end{equation}
and there are three cases. If $\Theta_i = 2\pi$, the faces fit exactly: around $i$ the mesh is flat, even if it is folded in space, and the ant cannot tell the vertex from a point of a plane. If $\Theta_i < 2\pi$, there is a gap: the vertex is the tip of a cone, like the apex of a pyramid or a vertex of a mesh of a sphere. If $\Theta_i > 2\pi$, the faces overlap: the vertex is a saddle, like a point of a ruffled collar. The difference
\begin{equation}
\label{eqn:defect}
\kappa_i = 2\pi - \Theta_i
\end{equation}
is the \emph{angle defect}, the discrete counterpart of the Gaussian curvature: the total curvature $\int K\, dA$ of the neighborhood of $i$, concentrated at the vertex \cite{Sharp2019}. It is intrinsic, a sum of angles of triangles, which is the discrete form of Gauss's \emph{Theorema Egregium}.
\begin{figure}[t]
\centering
\adjustbox{max width=\linewidth}{\input{figures/fig_cone_rescale}}
\caption{The faces around a vertex, cut along the reference edge (blue, $\theta = 0$) and flattened. Five equilateral triangles: $\Theta_i = 300^\circ$, a gap of $60^\circ$, the angle defect. Rescaling every angle by $s_i = 2\pi/\Theta_i = 6/5$ closes the gap: every $60^\circ$ corner becomes $72^\circ$. Seven equilateral triangles: $\Theta_i = 420^\circ$, the seventh overlaps the first; rescaling by $6/7$ turns every corner into $51.4^\circ$.}
\label{fig:cone_rescale}
\end{figure}

The angle defects of a closed mesh add up to $2\pi \chi$, where $\chi = V - E + F$ is the Euler characteristic: this is the discrete \emph{Gauss--Bonnet theorem}, and its proof is a count. The angles of each triangle add up to $\pi$, so $\sum_i \Theta_i = \pi F$. Each triangle has three edges and each edge is shared by two triangles, so $E = \frac32 F$. Then
\begin{equation*}
\sum_i \kappa_i = 2\pi V - \pi F = 2\pi \big(V - \tfrac32 F + F\big) = 2\pi (V - E + F).
\end{equation*}
On a mesh with boundary the same count works with $\pi - \Theta_i$ at the boundary vertices, the discrete geodesic curvature of the boundary. The program checks it on the cactus: the angle defects add up to $12.566371 = 4\pi$, the value of a sphere.

\subsection{Why a vertex has no tangent plane}

The directions at a point of a smooth surface form a circle, a full turn of $2\pi$. The directions at the vertex $i$ of a mesh, as the ant measures them, form a circle of $\Theta_i$: walking once around $i$ at a small distance, the ant turns by $\Theta_i$, not by $2\pi$. So when $\Theta_i \neq 2\pi$ there is no plane whose directions match those of the vertex, and none is ``correct''. To build the finite element method we need a plane anyway, because the coordinates of the unknowns at the vertex must be added and multiplied as vectors of one plane. Some map from the circle of $\Theta_i$ to a circle of $2\pi$ must be chosen, and that choice is a modeling decision. Part~\ref{part:matrix} made it with the vertex normal. Here we make it with the angles alone.

\subsection{Directions at a vertex as angles}

Choose a reference edge $e_0$ leaving the vertex: we take the same edge \code{vi.edge} whose projection is the vertex frame vector $t_i$ of Part~\ref{part:matrix}. Walk counterclockwise around the vertex, from one outgoing edge to the next, $e_0, e_1, e_2, \ldots$, and assign to each outgoing edge its \emph{cone angle}, the angle the ant measures from $e_0$:
\begin{equation}
\label{eqn:cone_angle}
\theta(e_0) = 0, \qquad \theta(e_{m+1}) = \theta(e_m) + \alpha_m ,
\end{equation}
where $\alpha_m$ is the angle at $i$ of the face between $e_m$ and $e_{m+1}$. Every direction at the vertex points into some corner and has a cone angle $\theta$ in $[0, \Theta_i)$; after a full turn $\theta$ reaches $\Theta_i$, which is again the direction of $e_0$. (The program walks clockwise, because that is the order of the half-edge iterator, and subtracts; its angles differ from these by $\Theta_i$, the same direction.)

Now rescale. Define the \emph{scale} of the vertex and the \emph{angle} of a direction in the plane of the vertex,
\begin{equation}
\label{eqn:scale}
s_i = \frac{2\pi}{\Theta_i}, \qquad \varphi = s_i\, \theta .
\end{equation}
As $\theta$ runs over $[0, \Theta_i)$, $\varphi$ runs over $[0, 2\pi)$: a full turn. This is our tangent plane of the vertex: a plane whose directions are the angles $\varphi$, with $\varphi = 0$ along the reference edge. A tangent vector at $i$ is a length and an angle $\varphi$, that is, the complex number $\psi = |U|\, e^{\mathsf T \varphi}$ of Part~\ref{part:ga}, the spinor coordinate of $U$ in the frame of the reference edge. Rescaling stretches every corner by the same factor $s_i$, so the corners tile the plane without gap or overlap (Figure~\ref{fig:cone_rescale}). The angle defect has not disappeared: it has been spread evenly over the corners, each corner absorbing the share $(s_i - 1)\, \alpha_m$ of it, proportional to its angle. On a fine mesh of a smooth surface $\Theta_i \to 2\pi$, $s_i \to 1$, and the stretching vanishes.

At a boundary vertex the directions do not form a circle but an arc, from the boundary edge on one side to the boundary edge on the other: there is no loop to close, and we do not rescale, $s_i = 1$. (Sharp, Soliman and Crane \cite{Sharp2019} use $s_i = \pi/\Theta_i$ instead, so that the boundary becomes a straight line in the plane of the vertex. Our choice keeps a flat sheet exactly flat: the corner of a rectangle has $\Theta_i = \pi/2$, and $\pi/\Theta_i$ would double its angles.)

\subsection{What the vertex normal does instead}

The vertex plane of Part~\ref{part:ga} solves the same problem in another way. Each face is rolled into the plane orthogonal to $n_i$ by the rotor $Q_{i \to f}$ of~(\ref{eqn:ga_Q}). A rotation preserves angles, so every corner keeps its true angle $\alpha_{i,f}$ in the vertex plane, and the rolled corners together cover $\Theta_i$, not $2\pi$. They cannot tile the plane: they leave gaps between neighbors when $\Theta_i < 2\pi$ and overlap when $\Theta_i > 2\pi$, and the gaps add up to the angle defect. Where the gaps fall depends on how each face is tilted with respect to the vertex normal, an extrinsic fact. The rescaling of~(\ref{eqn:scale}) spreads the defect in proportion to the angles, an intrinsic fact. Even at a flat vertex, $\Theta_i = 2\pi$, the rolled corners fit edge to edge only if all the faces are rolled about one line, as along a straight fold; otherwise they leave gaps and overlaps that cancel in total but not one by one, which is what happens on the Miura-ori of Figure~\ref{fig:miura}. On a symmetric vertex the two planes agree; on any other they differ, as the next two examples show.

\begin{example}
\label{ex:int_pyramid}
The apex of a pyramid over a regular pentagon of radius $1$, at the height $h = 0.6180$ that makes the five faces equilateral. Each corner at the apex is $60^\circ$, so $\Theta_i = 300^\circ$, $\kappa_i = 60^\circ$ and $s_i = 1.2$; the vertex normal is vertical, by symmetry. The program prints, for the corners in counterclockwise order from the reference edge:
\begin{center}
\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{c c | c c | c c c}
\toprule
& & \multicolumn{2}{c|}{intrinsic} & \multicolumn{3}{c}{extrinsic (rolled)} \\
corner & $\alpha$ & from & width & from & width & gap \\
\midrule
0 & $60^\circ$ & $0^\circ$ & $72^\circ$ & $6^\circ$ & $60^\circ$ & $12^\circ$ \\
1 & $60^\circ$ & $72^\circ$ & $72^\circ$ & $78^\circ$ & $60^\circ$ & $12^\circ$ \\
$\vdots$ & & & & & & \\
4 & $60^\circ$ & $288^\circ$ & $72^\circ$ & $294^\circ$ & $60^\circ$ & $12^\circ$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The intrinsic plane stretches each corner from $60^\circ$ to $72^\circ$. The extrinsic plane keeps each at $60^\circ$ and leaves five gaps of $12^\circ$, again $60^\circ$ in total. The middle directions of the corners, their bisectors, are the same in both: $36^\circ, 108^\circ, \ldots$ Since the connection of the next section matches bisectors, the two connections are equal at this vertex; the program finds them equal to rounding.
\end{example}

\begin{example}
\label{ex:int_leaning}
Move the apex of Example~\ref{ex:int_pyramid} by $0.4$ towards one corner of the pentagon. The corners become $70.49^\circ$, $70.49^\circ$, $51.37^\circ$, $46.81^\circ$, $51.37^\circ$, with $\Theta_i = 290.53^\circ$, $\kappa_i = 69.47^\circ$ and $s_i = 1.2391$. The vertex normal is still vertical: the sum of the cross products $(x_k - x_i) \times (x_{k+1} - x_i)$ over the faces, which~(\ref{eqn:vertex_normal}) averages, does not depend on $x_i$ at all (the terms with $x_i$ cancel around the ring), and equals twice the vector area of the pentagon. Figure~\ref{fig:pyramid} shows where each method puts the corners:
\begin{center}
\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{c c | c c | c c c}
\toprule
& & \multicolumn{2}{c|}{intrinsic} & \multicolumn{3}{c}{extrinsic (rolled)} \\
corner & $\alpha$ & from & width & from & width & gap \\
\midrule
0 & $70.488^\circ$ & $0^\circ$ & $87.341^\circ$ & $13.146^\circ$ & $70.488^\circ$ & $23.662^\circ$ \\
1 & $70.488^\circ$ & $87.341^\circ$ & $87.341^\circ$ & $107.296^\circ$ & $70.488^\circ$ & $15.181^\circ$ \\
2 & $51.373^\circ$ & $174.682^\circ$ & $63.655^\circ$ & $192.964^\circ$ & $51.373^\circ$ & $7.720^\circ$ \\
3 & $46.814^\circ$ & $238.337^\circ$ & $58.007^\circ$ & $252.057^\circ$ & $46.814^\circ$ & $7.720^\circ$ \\
4 & $51.373^\circ$ & $296.345^\circ$ & $63.655^\circ$ & $306.592^\circ$ & $51.373^\circ$ & $15.181^\circ$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
Both spread the defect of $69.47^\circ$ over the five corners, but differently. The intrinsic plane gives each corner $(s_i - 1)\alpha$: $16.85^\circ$, $16.85^\circ$, $12.28^\circ$, $11.19^\circ$, $12.28^\circ$, in proportion to the angles. The extrinsic plane leaves gaps of $23.66^\circ$, $15.18^\circ$, $7.72^\circ$, $7.72^\circ$, $15.18^\circ$, which depend on the tilt of the faces: the largest gap opens between the two faces on the side towards which the apex leans, which are tilted most with respect to the vertex normal. The bisectors now differ by $4.7^\circ, 11.5^\circ, 12.1^\circ, 8.1^\circ$ and $4.1^\circ$, and so do the two connections. For the ant both pyramids are cones of the same kind, and only the intrinsic plane treats them alike.
\end{example}
\begin{figure}[t]
\centering
\adjustbox{max width=\linewidth}{\input{figures/fig_projection}}
\caption{The leaning pyramid of Example~\ref{ex:int_leaning}, seen from its apex, corners counterclockwise from the reference edge (blue). Left: cut open and flattened, with a gap of $69.5^\circ$, the angle defect. Middle: the intrinsic vertex plane, every corner stretched by the same factor. Right: the extrinsic vertex plane of Part~\ref{part:ga}, every corner rolled rigidly into the plane orthogonal to the vertex normal; the defect falls into the gaps between them (red), unevenly.}
\label{fig:pyramid}
\end{figure}

%%% ----------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{The Connection from a Vertex into a Face}
\label{section:int_connection}

\subsection{What the finite element method needs}

The element matrix~(\ref{eqn:ga_element}) uses, for each corner of a face, a unit complex number $e^{\mathsf T \rho_i^f}$ with the meaning of~(\ref{eqn:ga_connection}): a vector of angle $\varphi$ in the plane of vertex $i$ is the vector of angle $\varphi + \rho_i^f$ in the face frame. In words, $\rho_i^f$ is where the reference direction of the vertex, $\varphi = 0$, lands in the face. Transport from vertex to face is a \emph{rotation}, multiplication by $e^{\mathsf T \rho_i^f}$, and it must be a rotation: the spinor hat function $\phi_i\, e^{\mathsf T \rho_i^f} \psi_i$ of Section~\ref{section:ga_fem} must be a rigid copy of the vertex vector in the face, so that the lengths and the angles of vectors, which the mass and the stiffness measure, are those of the vertex.

\subsection{The exact correspondence is not a rotation}

Look at one corner of the face $f$ at the vertex $i$. Let $e$ be the first edge of the corner in the counterclockwise order, with cone angle $\theta_e$ at the vertex~(\ref{eqn:cone_angle}) and angle $\beta_e$ in the face frame; let $\alpha$ be the angle of the corner. A direction inside the corner that makes the angle $u \in [0, \alpha]$ with $e$ has
\begin{equation*}
\text{in the face:}\quad \beta = \beta_e + u, \qquad \text{at the vertex:}\quad \varphi = s_i (\theta_e + u),
\end{equation*}
the first because the face is flat, the second by~(\ref{eqn:scale}). The rotation that would carry the vertex direction to the face direction is
\begin{equation*}
\beta - \varphi = (\beta_e - s_i \theta_e) + (1 - s_i)\, u ,
\end{equation*}
which changes across the corner, by $(1 - s_i)\, \alpha$ in total, unless $s_i = 1$. The exact correspondence stretches the corner; it is not a rotation. We need a single number $\rho_i^f$ for the corner.

\subsection{The best rotation: match the bisectors}

Choose $\rho$ to make the mismatch as small as possible on average over the corner, in the least squares sense:
\begin{equation*}
\min_\rho \int_0^\alpha \big(\rho - (\beta_e - s_i\theta_e) - (1 - s_i)\, u\big)^2\, du .
\end{equation*}
Setting the derivative with respect to $\rho$ to zero, $\rho$ is the mean of the linear function over $[0, \alpha]$, its value at the middle, $u = \alpha/2$:
\begin{equation}
\label{eqn:intrinsic_rho}
\rho_i^f = \Big(\beta_e + \frac{\alpha}{2}\Big) - s_i \Big(\theta_e + \frac{\alpha}{2}\Big).
\end{equation}
The two brackets are the angle of the bisector of the corner in the face and in the plane of the vertex: the intrinsic connection \emph{matches the bisectors}. The two edges of the corner are then off by $\mp (s_i - 1)\alpha/2$, half the stretching on each side (Figure~\ref{fig:corner_map}).

For a flat vertex, $s_i = 1$, (\ref{eqn:intrinsic_rho}) is $\rho_i^f = \beta_e - \theta_e$ with no mismatch at all: the vertex plane is the plane of the flattened faces, and transport into each face is the exact unfolding. So on every part of a mesh that is intrinsically flat, however it is folded in space, the intrinsic connection is the exact parallel transport. This is why it reproduces the field of the flat paper on the Miura-ori of Figure~\ref{fig:miura}.
\begin{figure}[t]
\centering
\adjustbox{max width=0.9\linewidth}{\input{figures/fig_corner_map}}
\caption{The second corner of the cone of Figure~\ref{fig:cone_rescale} ($\theta_e = 60^\circ$, $\alpha = 60^\circ$, $s_i = 1.2$) with the face frame along its first edge, $\beta_e = 0$. Left: in the face, the directions of the corner run from $0^\circ$ to $60^\circ$. Right: in the plane of the vertex, the corner is the sector from $72^\circ$ to $144^\circ$. The connection~(\ref{eqn:intrinsic_rho}), $\rho_i^f = 30^\circ - 108^\circ = -78^\circ$, carries bisector to bisector; the face's edges land $6^\circ$ inside the sector on each side.}
\label{fig:corner_map}
\end{figure}

\begin{example}
\label{ex:int_rho}
In the cone of five equilateral triangles, the corner of Figure~\ref{fig:corner_map} has $\theta_e = 60^\circ$ and $\alpha = 60^\circ$; with the face frame along its first edge, $\beta_e = 0$. Then~(\ref{eqn:intrinsic_rho}) gives $\rho_i^f = (0 + 30^\circ) - 1.2\,(60^\circ + 30^\circ) = 30^\circ - 108^\circ = -78^\circ$: the reference direction of the vertex lands at $-78^\circ$ in the face, and the bisector of the vertex sector, at $108^\circ$, lands at $108^\circ - 78^\circ = 30^\circ$, the bisector of the face corner.
\end{example}

\subsection{Going around the vertex}

The transport from a face $f$ into its neighbor $g$ around the vertex $i$ can be done in two ways: by unfolding $g$ onto $f$ across their common edge, the exact transport, or through the plane of the vertex, from $f$ to the vertex with $e^{-\mathsf T\rho_i^f}$ and from the vertex into $g$ with $e^{\mathsf T\rho_i^g}$, which is what the finite element method does. Unfolding preserves the cone angle: a direction of $f$ at the cone angle $\theta$ is carried to the direction of $g$ at the same cone angle. Through the vertex, by~(\ref{eqn:intrinsic_rho}), the direction is carried with the extra rotation
\begin{equation*}
(1 - s_i)\, \big[ (\theta_g + \tfrac12\alpha_g) - (\theta_f + \tfrac12\alpha_f) \big] = (1 - s_i)\, \tfrac12 (\alpha_f + \alpha_g),
\end{equation*}
$(1 - s_i)$ times the cone angle from the bisector of $f$ to that of $g$. Going once around the vertex, the cone angles between consecutive bisectors add up to $\Theta_i$, so the extra rotations add up to $(1 - s_i)\, \Theta_i = \Theta_i - 2\pi = -\kappa_i$. The unfolding, on the other hand, comes back rotated by the holonomy of the vertex, the angle defect $\kappa_i$ (Section~\ref{section:ga_intrinsic}). The two cancel: through the plane of the vertex, a vector comes back unchanged, as it must, since the vertex plane is one plane. The vertex plane does not ignore the curvature; it accounts for it by correcting the transport between consecutive faces, each by a share of the defect proportional to the angle crossed.

In the language of moving frames (Section~\ref{section:map_cartan}), this part uses a frame that is constant on each face, the $x$ axis of its layout, and one frame at each vertex, the reference edge of its stretched cone. The connection form vanishes inside every face, and the corner angles $\rho_i^f$ are the \emph{transition functions} between the frame of a vertex and the frames of its faces, the discrete counterpart of the change of frame~(\ref{eqn:ga_gauge}).

%%% ----------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{The Finite Element Method, Unchanged}
\label{section:int_fem}

With~(\ref{eqn:heron}), (\ref{eqn:intrinsic_gradient}) and~(\ref{eqn:intrinsic_rho}), the element matrix~(\ref{eqn:ga_element}) is computed without leaving the surface:
\begin{equation}
\label{eqn:intrinsic_element}
\mathrm L^f_{ij} = A_f\, (\nabla\phi_i \cdot \nabla\phi_j)\; e^{\mathsf T (\rho_j^f - \rho_i^f)}, \qquad \mathrm M^f_{ii} = \frac{A_f}{3} ,
\end{equation}
with $A_f$ from Heron's formula, the gradients from the layout, and the connection from the bisectors. The rest is Part~\ref{part:ga} without a single change: the assembly into the $n \times n$ Hermitian matrix, the scaling by the lumped mass, the Dirichlet row of the source, the sparse LU factorization and the solution. The transport between two vertices of a face is still $e^{\mathsf T(\rho_j^f - \rho_i^f)}$, in which the arbitrary angle of the face frame cancels.

\paragraph{The same gauge as Part~\ref{part:matrix}.} The reference direction $\varphi = 0$ of a vertex is its reference edge, the same edge whose projection is the frame vector $t_i$ of Part~\ref{part:matrix}. So both methods read a spinor coordinate as an angle from the same edge, and the value prescribed at the source, $e^{\mathsf T\, 2\pi/3}$, means in both ``$120^\circ$ from the reference edge'' in each method's own vertex plane. The two solutions can be compared number by number, as Section~\ref{section:int_experiments} does.

\paragraph{Drawing the result.} The positions of the vertices return only at the very end, to draw. To draw the coordinate $\psi_i$, take the cone angle $\theta = \arg(\psi_i) / s_i$, find the corner that contains it, and draw the direction in the plane of that face, turned from the first edge of the corner by $\theta - \theta_e$ (the function \code{lift}).

\paragraph{The code.} \code{IntrinsicConnection.h} computes the geometry. Heron's formula~(\ref{eqn:heron}) and the law of cosines~(\ref{eqn:law_of_cosines}):

The layout~(\ref{eqn:layout}), the angles and the gradients~(\ref{eqn:intrinsic_gradient}) of a face:
