%%% ----------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Experiments}
\label{section:int_experiments}

\code{ga\_fem} runs the vector heat method with both connections, the extrinsic one of Part~\ref{part:ga} and the intrinsic one of this part, on three folded sheets, five spheres and the cactus, with the same time step $\Delta t = h^2$ ($h$ the mean edge length) and the same solver.

\paragraph{How the error is measured.} A vertex has no tangent plane (Section~\ref{section:int_vertex}), so a vector at a vertex cannot be compared with an exact vector without choosing one, and any choice would favor one of the two methods. We compare in the faces instead. For each corner $(i, f)$, each method puts the vector of its solution at $i$ into the face $f$ with its own connection, $t_f\, e^{\mathsf T (\arg u_i + \rho_i^f)}$: this is the vector the finite element method actually uses in that face. We measure its angle to the exact vector of the face, at every corner of every face. The exact vectors are computed independently of both methods: on a flat or folded sheet, by carrying the source vector from face to face across the edges with the rotation about the shared edge, the unfolding of Section~\ref{section:ga_intrinsic}, which on an intrinsically flat mesh does not depend on the path; on the sphere, by transport along the great circle to the vertex, followed by the smallest rotation from the tangent plane of the sphere into the face. The source vector is the vector each method puts into the first face of the source vertex.

\paragraph{Folded sheets: exact bendings.} Three sheets, all intrinsically flat, every interior angle sum $2\pi$ to $10^{-13}$ degrees: a flat sheet of $48 \times 16$ squares, each cut by a diagonal into two right isosceles triangles (833 vertices); the same sheet folded along every fourth column of vertices by $30^\circ$, into twelve flat strips, almost a closed twelve-sided prism (parallel folds); and the Miura-ori of Figure~\ref{fig:miura}, $24 \times 16$ parallelograms, $X_{jk} = P_j + Q_k$ with the zigzags $P_j = (j u, 0, (j \bmod 2)\, w)$ and $Q_k = ((k \bmod 2)\, r, k t, 0)$. (Every face of such a net is a parallelogram, and at every vertex the four angles come in two pairs $\arccos(x) + \arccos(-x) = \pi$, so they add up to $2\pi$ exactly.) The exact answer on each is the field of the flat paper.
\begin{center}
\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{l c c c}
\toprule
& extrinsic, mean & extrinsic, max & intrinsic, max \\
\midrule
flat sheet & $7 \cdot 10^{-15}$ & $5 \cdot 10^{-14}$ & $7 \cdot 10^{-13}$ \\
parallel folds & $1 \cdot 10^{-14}$ & $4 \cdot 10^{-14}$ & $3 \cdot 10^{-13}$ \\
Miura-ori & $7.42^\circ$ & $21.9^\circ$ & $2 \cdot 10^{-13}$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The intrinsic method is exact on all three, as Section~\ref{section:int_connection} predicts: every vertex has $s_i = 1$, and every transport into a face is an unfolding. The extrinsic method is exact on the parallel folds too, because there every face is rolled into the vertex plane about the fold line, and rolling about the fold is unfolding. On the Miura-ori the faces around a vertex are rolled about four different axes, the rolled corners keep their angles but do not fit edge to edge, and the transports are wrong by several degrees at every vertex; the errors add up along the paths from the source.

\paragraph{The sphere: convergence.} On the geodesic spheres of Example~\ref{ex:sphere}, refined from 42 to 10242 vertices, we measure at every corner within three quarters of the way to the antipode (the antipode itself is the one point where the shortest path is not unique):
\begin{center}
\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{r r r | c c | c c}
\toprule
& & & \multicolumn{2}{c|}{extrinsic} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{intrinsic} \\
vertices & $h$ & largest $\kappa_i$ & mean & max & mean & max \\
\midrule
42 & 0.5823 & $17.72^\circ$ & $0.277^\circ$ & $1.340^\circ$ & $0.331^\circ$ & $1.502^\circ$ \\
162 & 0.2993 & $4.71^\circ$ & $0.209^\circ$ & $0.888^\circ$ & $0.210^\circ$ & $0.881^\circ$ \\
642 & 0.1507 & $1.30^\circ$ & $0.111^\circ$ & $0.473^\circ$ & $0.111^\circ$ & $0.492^\circ$ \\
2562 & 0.0755 & $0.34^\circ$ & $0.067^\circ$ & $0.307^\circ$ & $0.067^\circ$ & $0.306^\circ$ \\
10242 & 0.0378 & $0.08^\circ$ & $0.079^\circ$ & $0.506^\circ$ & $0.079^\circ$ & $0.504^\circ$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
On a curved surface neither connection is exact, and the two are practically equal from 162 vertices on (Figure~\ref{fig:intrinsic_plots}, left). Only on the coarsest sphere, whose vertices are cones with angle defects up to $17.7^\circ$, is the extrinsic one slightly better. The vertices of a geodesic sphere lie on the exact sphere, and their averaged normal is very nearly its exact normal, so Part~\ref{part:ga}'s vertex plane is nearly the true tangent plane of the smooth surface: information about the smooth sphere which the edge lengths do not contain, and which the intrinsic method, by design, does not use. As the mesh is refined the defects shrink, $s_i \to 1$, and the two vertex planes converge to the same plane. The slight increase of the error on the finest sphere is shared by both, so it belongs to the vector heat method with the time step $\Delta t = h^2$, not to the connection.

\paragraph{The cactus: how large is the choice?} On the cactus of Figure~\ref{fig:cactus} there is no exact answer, but we can measure how much the two connections disagree, corner by corner, with the same source value. The angle defects add up to $4\pi$, as the discrete Gauss--Bonnet theorem requires for a surface of genus zero; the largest is $63.7^\circ$, a sharp vertex. The angle between the two solutions has median $2.20^\circ$, $90\%$ of the corners are below $6.15^\circ$, and the largest difference is $23.4^\circ$, at a vertex where the field has decayed to $|u_i| = 1.1 \cdot 10^{-7}$. Figure~\ref{fig:intrinsic_plots}, right, shows that the large differences occur only where the field is small, on the far side of the cactus, where several shortest paths of nearly equal length compete and a small change of the connection decides between them. The few degrees elsewhere measure the size of the modeling choice that a coarse, strongly curved mesh leaves open at its vertices.
\begin{figure}[t]
\centering
\adjustbox{max width=\linewidth}{\input{figures/fig_intrinsic_plots}}
\caption{Left: the error of the vector heat method on geodesic spheres, against the number of vertices, with the extrinsic connection of Part~\ref{part:ga} (red) and the intrinsic one (green); mean (solid) and maximum (dashed), over all corners within three quarters of the way to the antipode. Right: on the cactus, the largest angle between the two solutions over the corners of each vertex, against the magnitude $|u_i|$ of the intrinsic solution: the two disagree strongly only where the field has almost vanished.}
\label{fig:intrinsic_plots}
\end{figure}

%%% ----------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Summary: Two Ways to Give a Vertex a Plane}
\label{section:int_summary}

Table~\ref{tab:intrinsic} lists what changes between Part~\ref{part:ga} and this part. Every ingredient of a face, the area, the gradients, the stiffness, was intrinsic all along, hidden in the Gram matrix $B^T B$. The one real difference is the tangent plane of a vertex, which a mesh does not have and which each method must invent: Part~\ref{part:ga} invents it from the vertex normal and rolls each face rigidly into it, leaving the angle defect in the gaps; this part invents it from the angles, stretching every corner by the same factor $2\pi/\Theta_i$. The connection then follows by matching the bisectors of the corners.

\begin{table}[t]
\centering
\caption{The ingredients of the element matrix in the extrinsic method of Part~\ref{part:ga} and in the intrinsic method of Part~\ref{part:intrinsic}.}
\label{tab:intrinsic}
\footnotesize
\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3pt}
\begin{tabular}{p{0.2\linewidth} p{0.37\linewidth} p{0.37\linewidth}}
\toprule
Ingredient & Extrinsic (Part~\ref{part:ga}) & Intrinsic (Part~\ref{part:intrinsic}) \\
\midrule
input & positions $x_i$, normals & edge lengths $\ell_{ij}$ \\
face geometry & $B$, $3 \times 2$ & metric $G_f = B^T B$ from lengths, (\ref{eqn:gram}) \\
area & $\frac12 |e_1 \times e_2|$ & Heron, (\ref{eqn:heron}) \\
gradients & reciprocal frame in space & quarter turn in the layout, (\ref{eqn:intrinsic_gradient}) \\
face frame & first edge, in space & first edge, the $x$ axis of the layout \\
vertex plane & orthogonal to the vertex normal $n_i$ & angles rescaled by $s_i = 2\pi/\Theta_i$, (\ref{eqn:scale}) \\
where the defect goes & gaps between rolled corners, by the tilt of the faces & into every corner, in proportion to its angle \\
connection $\rho_i^f$ & $e^{\mathsf T\rho} = t_f\, Q t_i \widetilde Q$, (\ref{eqn:ga_connection}) & bisector to bisector, (\ref{eqn:intrinsic_rho}) \\
intrinsically flat parts & exact for parallel folds only & exact \\
undefined when & $n_f = -n_i$ & never (non-degenerate triangles) \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

When to use which? The intrinsic connection is the natural one when the geometry is given by lengths, when the mesh is bent or folded (exactly, on every intrinsically flat part), when vertex normals are poor (thin or sharp features, noisy meshes), and it is the form used in geometry processing \cite{Knoppel2013, Sharp2019}; it needs no rotor of space and has no singular configuration. The extrinsic connection uses one more piece of information, the vertex normal, which can pay off slightly when the mesh coarsely samples a smooth surface whose normals it approximates well, as on the coarsest sphere above. On fine meshes of smooth surfaces the two agree, and their cost is the same.

\begin{rem}
\label{rem:gauge}
Readers who know the gauge theory of gravity in geometric algebra \cite{Doran2003} will recognize the two halves of this part. The metric $G_f = B^T B$, a rule for measuring lengths in coordinates, is what that theory encodes in its \emph{displacement gauge} field, the map from flat coordinates to the curved surface; the connection angle $\rho$, the rule for comparing frames, is its \emph{rotation gauge} $\omega \mathsf T$ of Section~\ref{section:ga_gauge}. On a mesh, the first is flat inside each face and given by three lengths, and the second is flat inside each face and carries all the curvature at the vertices, as the angle defects. Nothing in this part depends on that language.
\end{rem}


%%% ======================================================================
\part{Curvature on the Faces: the Connection as a Discrete 1-Form}
\label{part:edge}

Parts~\ref{part:matrix} to~\ref{part:intrinsic} carry a vector from a vertex into a face, and from there into the other vertices of the face. Inside a face that transport is flat, so going around a face changes nothing, and all the curvature of the mesh is left at the vertices, as the angle defects. This part does the opposite. It keeps the unknowns at the vertices, in the planes of Part~\ref{part:intrinsic}, but carries vectors directly from vertex to vertex, along the edges. A face then becomes a closed loop of three edges, and a vector carried around it comes back turned: the curvature moves from the vertices into the faces.

In the language of differential forms this is natural. The edge transports are one number per oriented edge, a discrete 1-form, the \emph{connection form}; the turning around a face is the sum of the 1-form around the boundary of the face, its exterior derivative, the \emph{curvature} 2-form; and Gauss--Bonnet becomes the theorem of Stokes. Section~\ref{section:edge_transport} builds the edge transport and its curvature. The finite element method then needs a new element, because a face with curvature has no flat frame: Section~\ref{section:edge_element} derives it, slowly. Part~\ref{part:dec} will show that discrete exterior calculus is this element with the curvature terms dropped.

%%% ----------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Parallel Transport Along the Edges}
\label{section:edge_transport}

\subsection{The transport along one edge}

An edge is a straight segment, and it lies in both of its faces: when the two faces are unfolded into one plane, the edge is a straight line in that plane. Parallel transport along a straight line in a plane keeps the angle between the vector and the line. So the natural transport along the edge from $a$ to $b$ is: \emph{keep the angle to the edge} (Figure~\ref{fig:edge_transport}).

In the vertex planes of Part~\ref{part:intrinsic}, the edge leaves $a$ in the direction $\varphi_{ab}$, the rescaled angle of the half edge $a \to b$, (\ref{eqn:scale}). A vector of angle $\theta$ at $a$ makes the angle $\gamma = \theta - \varphi_{ab}$ with the edge. At $b$, the edge leaves in the direction $\varphi_{ba}$, back towards $a$; the direction of travel, away from $a$, is $\varphi_{ba} + \pi$. The transported vector makes the same angle $\gamma$ with the direction of travel, so its angle at $b$ is $\varphi_{ba} + \pi + \gamma = \theta + \rho_{ab}$, with
\begin{equation}
\label{eqn:edge_transport}
\rho_{ab} = \varphi_{ba} + \pi - \varphi_{ab} .
\end{equation}
In spinor coordinates the transport is the multiplication $\psi \mapsto r_{ab}\, \psi$, $r_{ab} = e^{\mathsf T \rho_{ab}}$. Transporting back undoes it: $\rho_{ba} = \varphi_{ab} + \pi - \varphi_{ba} = -\rho_{ab} + 2\pi$, so $r_{ba} = \overline{r_{ab}}$. One number per edge, which changes sign with the orientation of the edge: this is what a discrete 1-form is.
\begin{figure}[t]
\centering
\adjustbox{max width=0.75\linewidth}{\input{figures/fig_edge_transport}}
\caption{Transport along the edge from $a$ to $b$ keeps the angle $\gamma$ between the vector and the edge. Each vertex measures angles from its own reference direction ($\varphi = 0$, gray); the edge points along $\varphi_{ab}$ at $a$ and along $\varphi_{ba} + \pi$ at $b$, so the transport turns the coordinate by $\rho_{ab} = \varphi_{ba} + \pi - \varphi_{ab}$.}
\label{fig:edge_transport}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Going around a face: the holonomy}

Carry a vector around the face $f$ with vertices $a, b, c$ in counterclockwise order: from $a$ to $b$, to $c$ and back to $a$. It comes back multiplied by $e^{\mathsf T \Omega_f}$, with $\Omega_f = \rho_{ab} + \rho_{bc} + \rho_{ca}$, the \emph{holonomy} of the face. Collect the terms of~(\ref{eqn:edge_transport}) vertex by vertex:
\begin{equation*}
\Omega_f = (\varphi_{ac} - \varphi_{ab}) + (\varphi_{ba} - \varphi_{bc}) + (\varphi_{cb} - \varphi_{ca}) + 3\pi .
\end{equation*}
Each bracket is the angle, in the plane of one vertex, between the two edges of its corner: at $a$, the edge $a \to b$ comes first counterclockwise, then $a \to c$, and by~(\ref{eqn:cone_angle}) and~(\ref{eqn:scale}) $\varphi_{ac} - \varphi_{ab} = s_a \alpha_a$, the rescaled angle of the corner. So $\Omega_f = s_a\alpha_a + s_b\alpha_b + s_c\alpha_c + 3\pi$, and since $\alpha_a + \alpha_b + \alpha_c = \pi$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eqn:face_holonomy}
\Omega_f = (s_a - 1)\,\alpha_a + (s_b - 1)\,\alpha_b + (s_c - 1)\,\alpha_c \qquad (\text{up to } 4\pi,\ \text{two full turns}).
\end{equation}
If the three vertices are flat, $s = 1$, the face has no holonomy, as a triangle of the plane should not. Otherwise each vertex $v$ gives the face the share $(s_v - 1)\alpha_v$ of its angle defect: summing over the faces around $v$, the shares add up to $(s_v - 1)\Theta_v = 2\pi - \Theta_v = \kappa_v$. So the face holonomies are the vertex curvatures of Part~\ref{part:intrinsic}, redistributed to the faces in proportion to the angles (Figure~\ref{fig:pyramid_faces}). Adding them over a closed mesh gives $\sum_f \Omega_f = \sum_v \kappa_v = 2\pi\chi$: the discrete Gauss--Bonnet theorem again. In the language of forms, the holonomy is the sum of the connection 1-form around the boundary of the face, which is how the exterior derivative of a discrete 1-form is defined (Section~\ref{section:dec_forms}): $\Omega = d\rho$, and $\sum_f \Omega_f = 2\pi\chi$ is what Stokes's theorem says about it. The program checks on the cactus that the face holonomies add up to $4\pi$; the largest is $34.5^\circ$.
\begin{figure}[t]
\centering
\adjustbox{max width=0.85\linewidth}{\input{figures/fig_pyramid_faces}}
\caption{The upright pyramid of Example~\ref{ex:int_pyramid}, cut open. With the corner connections of Parts~\ref{part:ga} and~\ref{part:intrinsic} the curvature is at the apex, the angle defect $60^\circ$. With the edge connection each face holds $(s_i - 1)\alpha = 0.2 \cdot 60^\circ = 12^\circ$ of it, (\ref{eqn:face_holonomy}).}
\label{fig:pyramid_faces}
\end{figure}

\begin{example}
\label{ex:edge_pyramid}
On the upright pyramid of Example~\ref{ex:int_pyramid}, take the face with the apex $0$ and the base vertices $1$ and $2$. The program prints the angles of the half edges in the vertex planes and the transports~(\ref{eqn:edge_transport}):
\begin{center}
\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{c c c c}
\toprule
edge & $\varphi_{ab}$ & $\varphi_{ba}$ & $\rho_{ab} = \varphi_{ba} + 180^\circ - \varphi_{ab}$ \\
\midrule
$0 \to 1$ & $72^\circ$ & $300^\circ$ & $408^\circ \equiv 48^\circ$ \\
$1 \to 2$ & $240^\circ$ & $0^\circ$ & $-60^\circ$ \\
$2 \to 0$ & $300^\circ$ & $144^\circ$ & $24^\circ$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The three transports add up to $\Omega_f = 12^\circ$, which is~(\ref{eqn:face_holonomy}): the apex has $s = 1.2$ and the corner $60^\circ$, the base vertices are on the boundary, $s = 1$, so $\Omega_f = 0.2 \cdot 60^\circ$. The five faces hold $5 \cdot 12^\circ = 60^\circ$, the angle defect of the apex.
\end{example}

%%% ----------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{A Face with a Curved Connection}
\label{section:edge_element}

\subsection{Why a new element is needed}

The elements of Parts~\ref{part:matrix} to~\ref{part:intrinsic} carry the three vertex values into one flat frame of the face and interpolate them there. With the edge transports that is impossible: a flat frame would make the transports around the face compose to the identity, but they compose to $e^{\mathsf T\Omega_f}$. To interpolate consistently with the edge transports, the face itself must be given a connection with total curvature $\Omega_f$. The simplest choice spreads it evenly, with the constant density
\begin{equation}
\label{eqn:kappa}
\kappa = \frac{\Omega_f}{A_f} .
\end{equation}
This curved connection lives on a flat triangle and is not its Levi-Civita connection: it is a model, the one that agrees with the edge transports, as a small piece of a sphere ($\kappa > 0$) or of a saddle ($\kappa < 0$) would.

\subsection{A connection with constant curvature in the plane}

Work in the layout of the face, Section~\ref{section:int_triangle}, with the face frame along the $x$ axis, and write vectors of the face by their complex coordinates $u$. A connection is a rule $D_X u = X \cdot \nabla u - \mathsf T\, (A \cdot X)\, u$, the ordinary derivative corrected by an infinitesimal rotation, where $A$ is a vector field, the \emph{connection 1-form} of the face; it is the form~(\ref{eqn:ga_covariant}) of Part~\ref{part:ga}, with $\omega(X) = -A \cdot X$. A field is parallel along a curve $x(t)$ when $D_{\dot x} u = 0$, that is, $\dot u = \mathsf T\, (A \cdot \dot x)\, u$, so
\begin{equation*}
u(\text{end}) = e^{\mathsf T \int A \cdot dx}\; u(\text{start}).
\end{equation*}
The curvature of the connection is the curl of $A$, $\partial_x A_y - \partial_y A_x$: by Stokes's theorem the rotation around a closed curve is $\oint A \cdot dx = \int (\text{curl}\, A)\, dA$. A constant curvature $\kappa$ is obtained with
\begin{equation}
\label{eqn:symmetric_gauge}
A(x) = \frac{\kappa}{2}\, J(x - c), \qquad J(x, y) = (-y, x),
\end{equation}
for any point $c$, since $\text{curl}\, A = \frac{\kappa}{2} + \frac{\kappa}{2} = \kappa$. (A physicist recognizes the vector potential of a uniform magnetic field in the symmetric gauge: Part~\ref{part:ga} already noted that the connection Laplacian is a magnetic Laplacian.) Along the straight segment from $p$ to $q$, $x = p + t (q - p)$, the integrand $A \cdot dx = \frac{\kappa}{2}\, J(p - c) \cdot (q - p)\, dt$ is constant, and $J(p - c) \cdot (q - p) = (p - c) \times (q - c)$, twice the signed area of the triangle $c, p, q$. So
\begin{equation}
\label{eqn:segment_transport}
\text{transport from } p \text{ to } q \text{ along the segment} = e^{\mathsf T \kappa\, \mathcal A(c, p, q)} ,
\end{equation}
where $\mathcal A(c, p, q)$ is the signed area, positive when $c, p, q$ are counterclockwise. Around the face, the three signed areas $\mathcal A(c, \bar x_a, \bar x_b)$, $\mathcal A(c, \bar x_b, \bar x_c)$, $\mathcal A(c, \bar x_c, \bar x_a)$ add up to $A_f$ for any $c$, and the holonomy is $\kappa A_f = \Omega_f$, as required (Figure~\ref{fig:face_holonomy}).
\begin{figure}[t]
\centering
\adjustbox{max width=0.45\linewidth}{\input{figures/fig_face_holonomy}}
\caption{A vector (blue) carried around a face with the curved connection~(\ref{eqn:symmetric_gauge}), here with an exaggerated $\Omega_f = 45^\circ$ and $c$ at the centroid: along each edge it turns steadily, by a third of $\Omega_f$, and it comes back to $a$ turned by $\Omega_f$ (red).}
\label{fig:face_holonomy}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Matching the vertex planes}

The vertex values $\psi_a$ live in the vertex planes. In the face they become $u_a = e^{\mathsf T\sigma_a} \psi_a$, with one angle $\sigma_a$ per corner, like $\rho_i^f$ of Part~\ref{part:intrinsic}. The angles must make the two transports along each edge agree: carrying $\psi_a$ to $b$ along the edge and then into the face must give the same as carrying it into the face and then along the segment, $e^{\mathsf T\sigma_b} r_{ab}\, \psi_a = e^{\mathsf T \kappa \mathcal A(c, \bar x_a, \bar x_b)} e^{\mathsf T\sigma_a} \psi_a$, that is,
\begin{equation}
\label{eqn:sigma}
\sigma_b - \sigma_a = \kappa\, \mathcal A(c, \bar x_a, \bar x_b) - \rho_{ab}
\end{equation}
for the three edges. Adding the three equations gives $0 = \kappa A_f - \Omega_f$, which holds by~(\ref{eqn:kappa}): the equations are consistent, and they fix the $\sigma_a$ up to one common angle, the arbitrary angle of the face frame, which will cancel.

\subsection{The trial functions}

Inside the face, interpolate the vertex values after carrying each one to the point $x$ along the straight segment from its vertex:
\begin{equation}
\label{eqn:curved_trial}
u(x) = \sum_a \phi_a(x)\, e^{\mathsf T\theta_a(x)}\, u_a, \qquad \theta_a(x) = \kappa\, \mathcal A(c, \bar x_a, x).
\end{equation}
At the vertex $b$ it takes the value $u_b$, since $\phi_a(\bar x_b) = \delta_{ab}$ and $\theta_b(\bar x_b) = 0$. On the edge from $a$ to $b$ only $\phi_a$ and $\phi_b$ are nonzero, and if the two values agree, $u_b$ being the transport of $u_a$, the interpolant is the parallel field along the edge (the two transported values coincide on the segment, because the signed areas add up along it). So the trace of $u$ on an edge depends only on the two vertex values and the transport of that edge, which both faces share: the element is conforming. (Along a curve a connection can always be written without rotation, so the two faces' different frames on the edge are related by a change of gauge.) In Cartan's terms (Section~\ref{section:map_cartan}), the face carries the frame of its layout with the connection form $\omega(X) = -A \cdot X$, whose curvature is $d\omega = -\operatorname{curl} A\; dA = -\kappa\, dA$: a moving frame whose curvature is prescribed, rather than computed from a curved geometry. The angles $\sigma_a$ are the transitions from the vertex frames into it, and $\theta_a(x)$ is the angle of parallel transport in it. Section~\ref{section:map_cartan} replaces the prescribed curvature by the true curvature of a curved element.

\subsection{The covariant derivative of a trial function}

By the product rule, with $D = \nabla - \mathsf T A$,
\begin{equation*}
D\big(\phi_a e^{\mathsf T \theta_a}\big) = e^{\mathsf T\theta_a} \big( \nabla\phi_a + \mathsf T \phi_a\, (\nabla\theta_a - A) \big).
\end{equation*}
Now $\theta_a(x) = \frac{\kappa}{2} (\bar x_a - c) \times (x - c)$ is linear in $x$, with gradient $\frac{\kappa}{2} J(\bar x_a - c)$, and by~(\ref{eqn:symmetric_gauge})
\begin{equation}
\label{eqn:w}
\nabla\theta_a - A = \frac{\kappa}{2} J(\bar x_a - c) - \frac{\kappa}{2} J(x - c) = \frac{\kappa}{2} J(\bar x_a - x) =: w_a(x),
\end{equation}
which no longer depends on $c$. It vanishes at the vertex $a$: near its own vertex, the carried value is parallel.

\subsection{The element matrix}

The Dirichlet energy of the face is $E_f = \int_f |D u|^2\, dA = \sum_{a, b} \overline{\psi_a}\, \mathrm L^f_{ab}\, \psi_b$, with
\begin{equation*}
\mathrm L^f_{ab} = \int_f \overline{e^{\mathsf T(\sigma_a + \theta_a)}}\; e^{\mathsf T(\sigma_b + \theta_b)}\; \overline{(\nabla\phi_a + \mathsf T\phi_a w_a)} \cdot (\nabla\phi_b + \mathsf T\phi_b w_b)\, dA .
\end{equation*}
The phase is $\sigma_b - \sigma_a + \theta_b - \theta_a$. By~(\ref{eqn:sigma}) and~(\ref{eqn:curved_trial}) it equals
\begin{equation*}
\kappa \big[ \mathcal A(c, \bar x_a, \bar x_b) + \mathcal A(c, \bar x_b, x) - \mathcal A(c, \bar x_a, x) \big] - \rho_{ab} = \kappa\, \mathcal A(\bar x_a, \bar x_b, x) - \rho_{ab},
\end{equation*}
because the signed areas of the three triangles that $c$ makes with the sides of the triangle $\bar x_a, \bar x_b, x$ add up to the area of that triangle. The point $c$ is gone, and with $-\rho_{ab} \equiv \rho_{ba}$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eqn:curved_element}
\boxed{\;\mathrm L^f_{ab} = e^{\mathsf T\rho_{ba}} \int_f e^{\mathsf T\kappa\, \mathcal A(\bar x_a, \bar x_b, x)}\; \overline{(\nabla\phi_a + \mathsf T\phi_a w_a)} \cdot (\nabla\phi_b + \mathsf T\phi_b w_b)\; dA\;}
\end{equation}
The factor in front, $e^{\mathsf T\rho_{ba}}$, carries $\psi_b$ into the plane of $a$ along their common edge; the integral is the curved element in the frame of the face. Three properties follow at once.
\begin{enumerate}
\item The matrix is Hermitian, $\mathrm L^f_{ba} = \overline{\mathrm L^f_{ab}}$: exchanging $a$ and $b$ conjugates both the transport and the integrand, since $\mathcal A(\bar x_b, \bar x_a, x) = -\mathcal A(\bar x_a, \bar x_b, x)$.
\item Without curvature, $\kappa = 0$, the integral is $A_f\, \nabla\phi_a \cdot \nabla\phi_b$, and $\mathrm L^f_{ab} = e^{\mathsf T\rho_{ba}} A_f\, \nabla\phi_a \cdot \nabla\phi_b$: the element of Part~\ref{part:intrinsic} with the edge transport in place of the corner connections.
\item On the diagonal, $\mathrm L^f_{aa} = \int_f |\nabla\phi_a|^2 + \phi_a^2 |w_a|^2\, dA$: the curvature costs energy, $\frac{\kappa^2}{4}\int_f \phi_a^2\, |\bar x_a - x|^2\, dA$, the ``diamagnetic'' term of the magnetic Laplacian.
\end{enumerate}
The mass matrix is lumped as before, $\mathrm M_{aa} = A_f/3$, and the assembly, the Dirichlet row and the solution are those of Part~\ref{part:ga}.

\paragraph{Computing the integral.} The integrand is smooth: a polynomial of degree two in $x$ times $e^{\mathsf T\kappa\mathcal A}$, whose phase is linear in $x$. We integrate it with a Gauss rule on the triangle, seen as a square with one side collapsed: the barycentric coordinates $(1 - s,\ s(1 - t),\ st)$ with $s, t \in [0, 1]$, the area element $2 A_f\, s\, ds\, dt$, and $8$ Gauss--Legendre points in each of $s$ and $t$. On the cactus face with the largest holonomy, $34.5^\circ$, the rule agrees with a $16 \times 16$ rule to $7 \cdot 10^{-16}$.

\begin{example}
\label{ex:edge_element}
For the face of Example~\ref{ex:edge_pyramid}, an equilateral triangle of area $0.5984$ with $\Omega_f = 12^\circ = 0.2094$, $\kappa = 0.35$. The program prints the integrals of~(\ref{eqn:curved_element}), without the transports in front:
\begin{equation*}
\begin{pmatrix} 0.57805 & -0.28587 - 0.05025\mathsf T & -0.28587 + 0.05025\mathsf T \\ \cdot & 0.57805 & -0.28587 - 0.05025\mathsf T \\ \cdot & \cdot & 0.57805 \end{pmatrix},
\end{equation*}
the lower triangle by conjugation. The flat element of the same triangle has $0.57735$ on the diagonal and $-0.28868$ off it. The curvature adds $0.0007$ to the diagonal and turns each off-diagonal entry by about $10^\circ$, and its rows no longer add up to zero: a field that is ``constant'' in the face frame is not parallel in a curved face.
\end{example}

%%% ----------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Results}
\label{section:edge_results}

Section~\ref{section:dec_results} compares all the methods on the meshes of Section~\ref{section:int_experiments}. In short: on the flat and folded sheets and on the Miura-ori every vertex is flat, $s = 1$, every face holonomy vanishes, the curved element is the flat one with exact transports, and the method is exact to $10^{-12}$ degrees. On the spheres it is as accurate as the intrinsic method of Part~\ref{part:intrinsic}, within a few percent at every resolution: moving the curvature from the vertices into the faces neither helps nor hurts the vector heat method. On the cactus the two differ by a median of $2.1^\circ$, comparable to the difference between Parts~\ref{part:ga} and~\ref{part:intrinsic}: the choice of where the curvature of a coarse mesh lives is a modeling choice of that size. The value of this part is elsewhere: it is a conforming finite element method for the edge connection, and it explains the method of the next part.

%%% ======================================================================
\part{Discrete Exterior Calculus, Slowly}
\label{part:dec}

The vector heat method of Sharp, Soliman and Crane \cite{Sharp2019}, and the direction fields of Kn\"oppel et al.\ \cite{Knoppel2013} before it, discretize the connection Laplacian with the tools of \emph{discrete exterior calculus} (DEC), in a few lines that assume the reader knows them. This part builds those tools from the beginning, with one triangle as the running example, and then shows that the resulting operator is the finite element method of Part~\ref{part:edge} with its curvature terms dropped.

%%% ----------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Discrete Differential Forms}
\label{section:dec_forms}

\subsection{Store integrals, not values}

A differential $k$-form is something that is integrated over $k$-dimensional pieces: a 0-form (a function) is evaluated at points, a 1-form is integrated along curves, a 2-form over regions. The idea of DEC is to store a $k$-form on a mesh by its integrals over the $k$-dimensional pieces of the mesh: a discrete 0-form is a number at each vertex, a discrete 1-form a number on each oriented edge (its integral along the edge), a discrete 2-form a number on each face (its integral over the face). Such lists of numbers are called \emph{cochains}. Reversing the orientation of an edge changes the sign of its number, as reversing a path changes the sign of an integral.

\subsection{The exterior derivative is exact}

The reason for storing integrals is that the exterior derivative $d$ then needs no approximation at all, because of Stokes's theorem $\int_S d\eta = \int_{\partial S} \eta$. For a function $f$, the integral of $df$ along the edge from $a$ to $b$ is $f_b - f_a$: the fundamental theorem of calculus. For a 1-form $\eta$, the integral of $d\eta$ over the face $abc$ is $\eta_{ab} + \eta_{bc} + \eta_{ca}$: Green's theorem. So
\begin{equation}
\label{eqn:dec_d}
(d_0 f)_{ab} = f_b - f_a, \qquad (d_1 \eta)_{abc} = \eta_{ab} + \eta_{bc} + \eta_{ca} ,
\end{equation}
and $d_0$, $d_1$ are the incidence matrices of the mesh, with entries $0$ and $\pm 1$. Applying both to a function, $(d_1 d_0 f)_{abc} = (f_b - f_a) + (f_c - f_b) + (f_a - f_c) = 0$: the discrete counterpart of $d \circ d = 0$, ``the curl of a gradient vanishes'', holds exactly (Figure~\ref{fig:cochains}, left). The edge transports of Part~\ref{part:edge} are a discrete 1-form, and the face holonomy~(\ref{eqn:face_holonomy}) is its $d_1$.
\begin{figure}[t]
\centering
\adjustbox{max width=0.9\linewidth}{\input{figures/fig_cochains}}
\caption{Left: a function at the vertices, its exterior derivative on the edges (the differences), and its second derivative on the face, which is zero. Right: the same for vector coordinates, with the edge transports of Part~\ref{part:edge}: the differences must be taken after transport, and around a face they add up to $(1 - e^{\mathsf T\Omega_f})\psi_1$, the curvature.}
\label{fig:cochains}
\end{figure}

%%% ----------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{The Hodge Star: Where the Geometry Enters}
\label{section:dec_hodge}

\subsection{Why lengths and areas are needed}

The exterior derivative uses only how the mesh is connected; it knows no lengths. Lengths enter with the inner product of forms, which the Dirichlet energy $\int |\nabla f|^2$ needs. In the smooth setting this is the \emph{Hodge star} $\star$, which turns a $k$-form into a $(2 - k)$-form; on a surface, $\star$ of a 1-form is the same 1-form turned by a quarter turn, and $\int \eta \wedge \star\eta = \int |\eta|^2$. In DEC, $\star$ is a diagonal matrix that converts the integral of a form over a piece of the mesh into its integral over the \emph{dual} piece, and it is the only approximation in the method.

\subsection{The dual mesh}

Put a dual vertex at the circumcenter of every face; join the circumcenters of two faces that share an edge $e$ by the dual edge $e^*$, which crosses $e$ at a right angle (both circumcenters lie on the perpendicular bisector of $e$); the dual edges around a vertex $i$ bound its dual cell $i^*$ (Figure~\ref{fig:dual}). Every primal piece has a dual piece of complementary dimension: vertex and cell, edge and dual edge, face and dual vertex. The Hodge stars are the ratios of their sizes:
\begin{equation}
\label{eqn:hodge}
(\star_0)_{ii} = |i^*|, \qquad (\star_1)_{ee} = \frac{|e^*|}{|e|}, \qquad (\star_2)_{ff} = \frac{1}{A_f} .
\end{equation}
For instance $\star_1$ says: a 1-form whose integral along $e$ is $\eta_e$ is, near $e$, roughly constant with magnitude $\eta_e / |e|$ along $e$; turned by a quarter turn it points along $e^*$, and its integral along $e^*$ is $\eta_e\, |e^*| / |e|$.
\begin{figure}[t]
\centering
\adjustbox{max width=0.42\linewidth}{\input{figures/fig_dual}}\hfill
\adjustbox{max width=0.52\linewidth}{\input{figures/fig_cotan_dual}}
\caption{Left: the circumcentric dual of a one-ring: dual vertices at the circumcenters (blue dots), dual edges (dashed) orthogonal to the primal edges, and the dual cell $i^*$ of the center vertex (shaded); one edge $e$ and its dual $e^*$ in bold. Right: the dual edge of $e = ab$ runs from the circumcenter $O_f$ of one face to $O_g$ of the other, through the midpoint $m$; its two pieces are $\frac{\ell}{2}\cot\alpha$ and $\frac{\ell}{2}\cot\beta$, with $\alpha$ and $\beta$ the angles opposite to $e$.}
\label{fig:dual}
\end{figure}

\subsection{The length of a dual edge}

\begin{prop}
\label{prop:dual_edge}
The distance from the circumcenter of a triangle to the midpoint of its edge of length $\ell$ is $\frac{\ell}{2} \cot\alpha$, with $\alpha$ the angle opposite to the edge (negative, that is, on the other side of the edge, when $\alpha$ is obtuse). Hence $|e^*| = \frac{\ell}{2}(\cot\alpha + \cot\beta)$ and
\begin{equation}
\label{eqn:star1}
(\star_1)_{ee} = \frac12 (\cot\alpha + \cot\beta).
\end{equation}
\end{prop}
\begin{proof}
Let $O$ be the circumcenter, $a$ and $b$ the ends of the edge and $m$ its midpoint. By the inscribed angle theorem, the angle $aOb$ at the center is twice the angle $\alpha$ at the circumference, so the angle $aOm$ is $\alpha$. The triangle $aOm$ has a right angle at $m$ and the side $|am| = \ell/2$, so $|Om| = |am| / \tan\alpha = \frac{\ell}{2}\cot\alpha$. The dual edge is made of the two pieces from $O_f$ and $O_g$ to $m$.
\end{proof}
The dual edge of a boundary edge has only one piece, and its $\star_1$ only one cotangent. For $\star_0$ two choices are common: the area of the circumcentric dual cell, or a third of the area of each face around the vertex, the \emph{barycentric} cell. The second is the lumped mass of the finite element method, (\ref{eqn:lumped_mass}), and is the one we use in all five parts.

%%% ----------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{The Scalar Laplacian of DEC Is the Finite Element Laplacian}
\label{section:dec_scalar}

The Dirichlet energy of a function, in DEC, is the inner product of its derivative with itself: $\int |\nabla f|^2 \approx (d_0 f)^T \star_1 (d_0 f) = \sum_e (\star_1)_{ee} (f_b - f_a)^2$. Its matrix is
\begin{equation}
\label{eqn:dec_laplacian}
\mathrm L = d_0^T \star_1 d_0: \qquad \mathrm L_{ab} = -\tfrac12 (\cot\alpha + \cot\beta), \qquad \mathrm L_{aa} = -\textstyle\sum_{b \neq a} \mathrm L_{ab},
\end{equation}
for neighbors $a \neq b$, with $\alpha$ and $\beta$ the angles opposite to their edge,
and the Laplacian is $\Delta = -\star_0^{-1} \mathrm L$, with the inverse of $\star_0$ as the codifferential's ``division by area''. These are exactly the stiffness matrix of the linear finite element method, Proposition~\ref{prop:cotangent}, and its lumped mass: for scalar functions, DEC and linear finite elements with lumped mass are the same method, reached by two roads. The finite element road computes $\int \nabla\phi_a \cdot \nabla\phi_b$; the DEC road computes the ratio of a dual length to a primal one; Proposition~\ref{prop:dual_edge} is why they agree.

\begin{example}
\label{ex:dec_triangle}
For the triangle of Example~\ref{ex:triangle}, with angles $90^\circ$ at $x_1$, $35.26^\circ$ at $x_2$, $54.74^\circ$ at $x_3$, the edges $12$, $23$, $31$ are opposite to $x_3$, $x_1$, $x_2$, so (one face, one cotangent each)
\begin{equation*}
\star_1 = \operatorname{diag}\big(\tfrac12\cot 54.74^\circ,\ \tfrac12\cot 90^\circ,\ \tfrac12\cot 35.26^\circ\big) = \operatorname{diag}(0.3536,\ 0,\ 0.7071),
\end{equation*}
\begin{equation*}
d_0 = \begin{pmatrix} -1 & 1 & 0 \\ 0 & -1 & 1 \\ 1 & 0 & -1 \end{pmatrix} \quad (\text{rows: edges } 12, 23, 31;\ \text{columns: vertices } 1, 2, 3),
\end{equation*}
and $d_0^T \star_1 d_0$ is the stiffness matrix of Example~\ref{ex:triangle}, entry for entry (the program prints it). The edge $23$ is opposite to the right angle: it is a diameter of the circumcircle, the circumcenter is its midpoint $(1, 0.707107)$ in the layout, its dual edge piece has length zero, and so has its weight. The other two pieces have lengths $0.707107 = \frac{2}{2}\cot 54.74^\circ$ and $1 = \frac{\sqrt 2}{2}\cot 35.26^\circ$, as Proposition~\ref{prop:dual_edge} says.
\end{example}

%%% ----------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Vector Fields: the Covariant Exterior Derivative}
\label{section:dec_vector}

\subsection{Differences after transport}

A vector field at the vertices is a list of spinor coordinates $\psi_a$, each in the plane of its vertex. The difference $\psi_b - \psi_a$ means nothing, because the two numbers are angles from different reference directions. The difference that means something is the one taken after carrying $\psi_a$ to $b$ along the edge with the transport of Part~\ref{part:edge}:
\begin{equation}
\label{eqn:dnabla}
(d^\nabla \psi)_{ab} = \psi_b - r_{ab}\, \psi_a ,
\end{equation}
the \emph{covariant exterior derivative}, a discrete 1-form with values in the plane of the end vertex $b$. It is zero exactly when $\psi_b$ is the parallel transport of $\psi_a$. As a matrix it is $d_0$ with the entry $-1$ of each row replaced by $-r_{ab}$. In a moving frame (Section~\ref{section:map_cartan}), the equation of a parallel field, $d\psi + \omega\mathsf T\psi = 0$, integrates along the edge to $\psi_b = e^{-\mathsf T\int_a^b \omega}\, \psi_a$, (\ref{eqn:rho_omega}); so $d^\nabla\psi$ measures, at $b$, how far $\psi_b$ is from the value that the moving frame equation carries from $a$.

\subsection{The energy and the connection Laplacian}

The Dirichlet energy of the field is the same expression as for functions, with $d^\nabla$ in place of $d_0$:
\begin{equation}
\label{eqn:dec_energy}
E(\psi) = \sum_e (\star_1)_{ee}\, |\psi_b - r_{ab}\psi_a|^2 = (d^\nabla\psi)^H \star_1 (d^\nabla \psi),
\qquad
\mathrm L^\nabla = (d^\nabla)^H \star_1\, d^\nabla ,
\end{equation}
where $^H$ is the conjugate transpose. To read off the entries, expand one term. For complex numbers $|z - w|^2 = |z|^2 + |w|^2 - 2\,\mathrm{Re}(\bar z w)$, so with $z = \psi_b$ and $w = r_{ab}\psi_a$, and $|r_{ab}| = 1$,
\begin{equation*}
|\psi_b - r_{ab}\psi_a|^2 = |\psi_b|^2 + |\psi_a|^2 - 2\,\mathrm{Re}\,(\overline{\psi_b}\, r_{ab}\, \psi_a) = |\psi_b|^2 + |\psi_a|^2 - 2\,\mathrm{Re}\,(\overline{\psi_a}\, r_{ba}\, \psi_b) ,
\end{equation*}
where the last step takes the complex conjugate inside the real part, which does not change it, and uses $\overline{r_{ab}} = r_{ba}$. Now compare with a Hermitian form $\psi^H \mathrm L\psi = \sum_a \mathrm L_{aa}|\psi_a|^2 + \sum_{a \ne b} \overline{\psi_a}\, \mathrm L_{ab}\, \psi_b$, in which the two entries of an edge, $\mathrm L_{ab}$ and $\mathrm L_{ba} = \overline{\mathrm L_{ab}}$, contribute $\overline{\psi_a}\,\mathrm L_{ab}\psi_b + \overline{\psi_b}\,\overline{\mathrm L_{ab}}\,\psi_a = 2\,\mathrm{Re}(\overline{\psi_a}\,\mathrm L_{ab}\psi_b)$. Matching the terms of every edge, weighted by $(\star_1)_{ee}$, gives the entries
\begin{equation}
\label{eqn:dec_entries}
\mathrm L^\nabla_{aa} = \sum_{b} \tfrac12 (\cot\alpha_{ab} + \cot\beta_{ab}), \qquad \mathrm L^\nabla_{ab} = -\tfrac12 (\cot\alpha_{ab} + \cot\beta_{ab})\; r_{ba} ,
\end{equation}
the cotangent Laplacian with the transport $r_{ba}$, from $b$ into the plane of $a$, on every off-diagonal entry. This is the connection Laplacian of \cite{Knoppel2013, Sharp2019}. The backward Euler step is $(\star_0 + \Delta t\, \mathrm L^\nabla)\psi = \star_0 \psi^0$, and the rest of the program is unchanged.

\subsection{Curvature: $d^\nabla d^\nabla \neq 0$}

For functions $d_1 d_0 = 0$. For vector coordinates it fails, and the failure is the curvature. Extend $d^\nabla$ to 1-forms in the same spirit: on the face $abc$, carry the three edge values to the vertex $a$ and add them,
\begin{equation*}
(d^\nabla \eta)_{abc} = \eta_{ca} + r_{ca}\, \eta_{bc} + r_{ca} r_{bc}\, \eta_{ab}
\end{equation*}
($\eta_{ab}$ lives at $b$, so it is carried to $c$ and then to $a$; $\eta_{bc}$ lives at $c$; $\eta_{ca}$ at $a$). For $\eta = d^\nabla\psi$ the terms telescope:
\begin{equation*}
\begin{split}
(\psi_a - r_{ca}\psi_c) + r_{ca}(\psi_c - r_{bc}\psi_b) + r_{ca} r_{bc} (\psi_b - r_{ab}\psi_a) &= (1 - r_{ca} r_{bc} r_{ab})\, \psi_a \\
&= (1 - e^{\mathsf T\Omega_f})\, \psi_a .
\end{split}
\end{equation*}
So $d^\nabla d^\nabla = 1 - e^{\mathsf T\Omega_f}$ on every face (Figure~\ref{fig:cochains}, right): it vanishes exactly on the faces without holonomy. A connection is \emph{flat} when $d^\nabla d^\nabla = 0$, and the curvature is the obstruction to it, as in the smooth theory, where $d^\nabla d^\nabla$ is multiplication by the curvature 2-form.

\begin{example}
\label{ex:dec_curvature}
On the face of Example~\ref{ex:edge_pyramid}, $\Omega_f = 12^\circ$ and $1 - e^{\mathsf T\Omega_f} = 0.021852 - 0.207912\,\mathsf T$, of modulus $2\sin 6^\circ = 0.209057$. For the vertex values $\psi = (0.3 - 0.7\mathsf T,\ 1.1 + 0.2\mathsf T,\ -0.4 + 0.5\mathsf T)$ the program computes $d^\nabla$ twice and finds $-0.138982 - 0.077670\,\mathsf T$, which is $(1 - e^{\mathsf T\Omega_f})\,\psi_1$ to all digits.
\end{example}

%%% ----------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{DEC as a Finite Element Method}
\label{section:dec_fem}

\subsection{The linear element written edge by edge}

The rows of the scalar element matrix add up to zero, because the hat functions add up to one and their gradients to zero. So the scalar element energy can be written as a sum over the three edges of the face:
\begin{equation*}
\begin{split}
\sum_{a, b} \overline{u_a}\, A_f(\nabla\phi_a \cdot \nabla\phi_b)\, u_b &= \sum_{\text{edges } ab \text{ of } f} \big(-A_f\, \nabla\phi_a \cdot \nabla\phi_b\big)\, |u_b - u_a|^2 \\
&= \sum_{\text{edges } ab \text{ of } f} \tfrac12 \cot\alpha_{ab}\, |u_b - u_a|^2 ,
\end{split}
\end{equation*}
using $-A_f \nabla\phi_a \cdot \nabla\phi_b = \frac12\cot\alpha_{ab}$ (Proposition~\ref{prop:cotangent}). Summing over the two faces of each edge gives $\sum_e (\star_1)_{ee}\, |u_b - u_a|^2$: the DEC energy for functions.

\subsection{Where DEC puts the transports}

Now let the values live in the vertex planes, $u_a = e^{\mathsf T\sigma_a}\psi_a$ in a flat frame of the face. Then $|u_b - u_a| = |\psi_b - e^{\mathsf T(\sigma_a - \sigma_b)}\psi_a|$, and this is the DEC difference $|\psi_b - r_{ab}\psi_a|$ when $\sigma_a - \sigma_b = \rho_{ab}$ on every edge of the face. By~(\ref{eqn:sigma}) with $\kappa = 0$ that is possible only if $\Omega_f = 0$. DEC does not ask: it uses, edge by edge, the transport of that edge, as if each pair of vertices of the face could be compared in its own flat frame. In terms of Part~\ref{part:edge}, DEC is the curved element~(\ref{eqn:curved_element}) with $\kappa = 0$ inside the integral,
\begin{equation*}
\mathrm L^f_{ab} = e^{\mathsf T\rho_{ba}}\, A_f\, \nabla\phi_a \cdot \nabla\phi_b ,
\end{equation*}
and summing over the two faces of each edge gives exactly~(\ref{eqn:dec_entries}). The holonomy is kept, in the transports, but the curvature inside the faces is dropped: the factor $e^{\mathsf T\kappa\mathcal A}$ and the terms $\mathsf T\phi\, w$. Those terms are of the order of $\Omega_f$, so the two methods agree on intrinsically flat meshes, exactly, and on fine meshes of smooth surfaces, where $\Omega_f \to 0$; on coarse curved meshes DEC is a slightly cruder finite element method. In the finite element vocabulary, DEC commits a \emph{variational crime}: its energy is not the energy of any field in a conforming space, but it converges, because the crime vanishes with the curvature of the faces.

%%% ----------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{The Code and the Results}
\label{section:dec_results}

The DEC operator is built in \code{ga\_fem.cpp} exactly as written in~(\ref{eqn:dec_energy}): the matrix $d^\nabla$, one row per edge, the diagonal $\star_1$ and $\star_0$, and the product $(d^\nabla)^H \star_1 d^\nabla$ computed by Eigen:
\lstinputlisting[firstline=286, lastline=321, firstnumber=286]{../src/ga_fem.cpp}

The program runs the four connections on the meshes of Section~\ref{section:int_experiments}, with the same measurement, corner by corner in the faces; Parts~\ref{part:edge} and~\ref{part:dec} put a vertex vector into a face by keeping its angle to the edge of the corner. On the flat sheet, the parallel folds and the Miura-ori, the methods of Parts~\ref{part:intrinsic}, \ref{part:edge} and~\ref{part:dec} are all exact, to $10^{-12}$ degrees, and only Part~\ref{part:ga} fails on the Miura-ori ($7.42^\circ$ mean, $21.9^\circ$ max). On the spheres (Figure~\ref{fig:four_plots}), mean and largest error:
\begin{center}
\scriptsize

\begin{tabular}{r c | c c c c}
\toprule
& largest & II & III & IV & V \\
vertices & $\Omega_f$ & extrinsic & intrinsic & curved & DEC \\
\midrule
42 & $9.32^\circ$ & $0.277^\circ$ / $1.340^\circ$ & $0.331^\circ$ / $1.502^\circ$ & $0.332^\circ$ / $1.615^\circ$ & $0.409^\circ$ / $1.814^\circ$ \\
162 & $2.39^\circ$ & $0.209^\circ$ / $0.888^\circ$ & $0.210^\circ$ / $0.881^\circ$ & $0.209^\circ$ / $0.899^\circ$ & $0.233^\circ$ / $0.981^\circ$ \\
642 & $0.65^\circ$ & $0.111^\circ$ / $0.473^\circ$ & $0.111^\circ$ / $0.492^\circ$ & $0.109^\circ$ / $0.487^\circ$ & $0.119^\circ$ / $0.530^\circ$ \\
2562 & $0.17^\circ$ & $0.067^\circ$ / $0.307^\circ$ & $0.067^\circ$ / $0.306^\circ$ & $0.068^\circ$ / $0.308^\circ$ & $0.066^\circ$ / $0.308^\circ$ \\
10242 & $0.04^\circ$ & $0.079^\circ$ / $0.506^\circ$ & $0.079^\circ$ / $0.504^\circ$ & $0.080^\circ$ / $0.509^\circ$ & $0.078^\circ$ / $0.501^\circ$ \\

DEC is the least accurate on the coarse spheres, $23\%$ above the curved element on the coarsest and $11\%$ on the next, and the difference disappears with the face holonomies, as Section~\ref{section:dec_fem} predicts: it is the curvature inside the faces that DEC drops. On the cactus, whose faces have holonomies up to $34.5^\circ$, the curved element and DEC differ by a median of $3.6^\circ$ ($90\%$ of the corners below $6.2^\circ$), more than any other pair of methods: the intrinsic and curved methods differ by $2.1^\circ$, the extrinsic and intrinsic by $2.2^\circ$.

%%% ----------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Summary of the Five Parts}
\label{section:summary_all}

All five parts solve the same equation with the same unknowns, one complex number per vertex, the same lumped mass, the same time step and the same solver. They differ in one thing only: how a vector at one vertex is compared with a vector at another, and therefore where the curvature of the mesh is kept (Table~\ref{tab:five}).

\begin{table}[t]
\centering
\caption{The five discretizations of the connection Laplacian in this text.}
\label{tab:five}
\footnotesize
\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3pt}
\begin{tabular}{>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.16\linewidth} >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.17\linewidth} >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.17\linewidth} >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.19\linewidth} >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.19\linewidth}}
\toprule
& Parts I, II & Part III & Part IV & Part V (DEC) \\
\midrule
input & positions, normals & edge lengths & edge lengths & edge lengths \\
vertex plane & orthogonal to $n_i$ & angles $\times\, 2\pi/\Theta_i$ & angles $\times\, 2\pi/\Theta_i$ & angles $\times\, 2\pi/\Theta_i$ \\
transport & vertex $\to$ face, rolling & vertex $\to$ face, bisectors & vertex $\to$ vertex, edges & vertex $\to$ vertex, edges \\
curvature lives & at the vertices (gaps of rolled corners) & at the vertices (stretched corners) & in the faces, as a curved connection & in the faces, as holonomy only \\
element & flat, $A\nabla\phi\cdot\nabla\phi\, e^{\mathsf T\Delta\rho}$ & flat, same form & curved, (\ref{eqn:curved_element}), quadrature & flat with edge transports \\
exact on flat folded meshes & parallel folds only & yes & yes & yes \\
coarsest sphere, mean & $0.28^\circ$ & $0.33^\circ$ & $0.33^\circ$ & $0.41^\circ$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

For the vector heat method on reasonable meshes the four agree to a fraction of a degree, and the simplest, DEC, is a fine choice: its matrix is one product of three sparse matrices. The other parts explain what that matrix is. It is a finite element method (Part~\ref{part:dec}) that drops the curvature terms of Part~\ref{part:edge}; it works in the vertex planes of Part~\ref{part:intrinsic}; and those planes replace the vertex normals of Parts~\ref{part:matrix} and~\ref{part:ga}, which are exact on a folded flat mesh only when the folds are parallel. Part~\ref{part:map} puts all of this on one page, and goes further.


%%% ======================================================================
\part{How the Pieces Fit}
\label{part:map}

The five parts were written one after another, each answering a question that the previous one left open: Part~\ref{part:matrix} derived the method, Part~\ref{part:ga} asked what is intrinsic, Part~\ref{part:intrinsic} removed the extrinsic ingredient, Part~\ref{part:edge} moved the curvature into the faces, and Part~\ref{part:dec} explained discrete exterior calculus. Read in that order they are a story. Coming back to them after a long time, a reader needs something else: a map. What is the skeleton that all five share? Where exactly do they differ, and why there and nowhere else? How are the concepts (connection, connection form, holonomy, curvature, 1-forms, the Laplacians) related to each other? Which of the methods should be used for a new problem, and what can go wrong?

This part is that map. It adds no new discretization. It puts the existing ones side by side, proves two facts that join them, and places the whole family among the other ways of computing with vector fields on surfaces. The two facts are Proposition~\ref{prop:corner_edge}, which says exactly how the corner transport of Part~\ref{part:intrinsic} differs from the edge transport of Part~\ref{part:edge}, and the Weitzenb\"ock identity~(\ref{eqn:weitzenbock}), which says how the connection Laplacian of this text differs from the Hodge Laplacian of differential forms. Both are checked numerically by \code{ga\_fem}.

Every section begins with a short summary and points back to where the details are, so that the sections can be read in any order:
\begin{itemize}
\item the common skeleton of all the methods: Section~\ref{section:map_pipeline};
\item the three choices that make them different, and why there are only three: Section~\ref{section:map_choices};
\item where each method keeps the curvature of the mesh: Section~\ref{section:map_curvature};
\item corner transport against edge transport, exactly: Section~\ref{section:map_transport};
\item the connection form, in every part: Section~\ref{section:map_form};
\item Cartan's moving frames as the common language of Parts~\ref{part:matrix} to~\ref{part:edge}, and a sketch of a curved element of degree two built on them: Section~\ref{section:map_cartan};
\item vector-valued forms, scalar 1-forms, the Hodge Laplacian and the Weitzenb\"ock identity: Section~\ref{section:map_forms};
\item the standard method of numerical analysis, which needs no discrete connection at all: Section~\ref{section:map_extrinsic};
\item what matrices, geometric algebra and complex numbers each contribute: Section~\ref{section:map_notation};
\item one small mesh traced through every step with every method: Section~\ref{section:map_example};
\item the concept map, the master table and the list of symbols: Sections~\ref{section:map_concepts} and~\ref{section:map_tables};
\item which method to choose, how to extend it to a new problem, and the mistakes to avoid: Section~\ref{section:map_choose}.
\end{itemize}

%%% ----------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{One Pipeline for All the Methods}
\label{section:map_pipeline}

\paragraph{Summary.} All five parts run the same eight steps (Figure~\ref{fig:pipeline}). Five of them are identical in every part. The methods differ only in steps 2, 3 and 6: where the unknown vector of a vertex lives, how the vectors of two vertices are compared, and which element matrix integrates the energy.

\begin{figure}[t]
\centering
\adjustbox{max width=\linewidth}{\input{figures/fig_pipeline}}
\caption{The pipeline that every part of this text follows. The highlighted steps are the only ones in which the methods differ (Section~\ref{section:map_choices}); step 4 is not a choice but a consequence of steps 2 and 3.}
\label{fig:pipeline}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{The problem.} Given a tangent vector at one vertex of a triangle mesh, find at every other vertex the vector obtained by parallel transport along the shortest path. The vector heat method (Section~\ref{section:pde}) answers it by one backward Euler step of the heat equation for tangent fields, $(I + \Delta t\, \nabla^*\nabla)\, U = U^0$, followed by normalization. Everything in this text is a way of discretizing $\nabla^*\nabla$, the connection Laplacian, with one complex unknown per vertex.

\paragraph{Step 1: the geometry of the mesh.} What is read from the mesh. Parts~\ref{part:matrix} and~\ref{part:ga} read the positions $x_i$, and compute normals, frames and rotations of space from them. Parts~\ref{part:intrinsic} to~\ref{part:dec} read only the edge lengths $\ell_{ij}$. The difference is smaller than it looks: by Proposition~\ref{prop:metric_only}, everything a single face contributes (area, gradients of the hat functions, the scalar stiffness and mass) depends on the positions only through the Gram matrix $G_f = B^T B$, which the three lengths determine~(\ref{eqn:gram}). The positions carry, in addition, how the faces are tilted with respect to each other in space. That extra information is used in one place only: step 2 of Parts~\ref{part:matrix} and~\ref{part:ga}.

\paragraph{Step 2: the vertex planes.} The unknown at vertex $i$ is a tangent vector, stored as a complex number $\psi_i$, a length and an angle. An angle needs a plane to be measured in, and a vertex of a mesh has no tangent plane (Section~\ref{section:int_vertex}): its directions form a circle of total angle $\Theta_i$, not $2\pi$. So each method must \emph{invent} a plane for every vertex. Part~\ref{part:ga} takes the plane orthogonal to the vertex normal~(\ref{eqn:vertex_normal}) and rolls each face rigidly into it. Parts~\ref{part:intrinsic} to~\ref{part:dec} take the cone of directions and stretch every angle by $s_i = 2\pi/\Theta_i$~(\ref{eqn:scale}). In both, the angle $\varphi = 0$ is the reference edge \code{vi.edge}, so that $\psi_i$ means the same thing (``this many degrees from the reference edge'') in all the methods, and their solutions can be compared.

\paragraph{Step 3: the transport.} Two numbers $\psi_i$ and $\psi_j$ live in two different planes and cannot be subtracted until one of them is carried into the plane of the other. The rule that carries them is the discrete connection. Parts~\ref{part:matrix} to~\ref{part:intrinsic} carry each vertex value into each face around it, one angle $\rho_i^f$ per \emph{corner} $(i, f)$; two vertices of a face are then compared inside the face. Parts~\ref{part:edge} and~\ref{part:dec} carry a vertex value directly into the plane of a neighbor, one angle $\rho_{ab}$ per \emph{edge}. Section~\ref{section:map_transport} compares the two exactly.

\paragraph{Step 4: the curvature.} A transport that is followed around a closed loop comes back turned by some angle, its \emph{holonomy}, and that angle is the curvature enclosed by the loop. Which loops close and which do not is decided by steps 2 and 3, not chosen separately. The total is always the same, $2\pi\chi$ on a closed mesh, because of the discrete Gauss--Bonnet theorem (Section~\ref{section:int_vertex}); what changes from method to method is \emph{where} it is kept (Section~\ref{section:map_curvature}).

\paragraph{Step 5: the covariant derivative of a trial field.} The finite element method interpolates the vertex values inside each face and differentiates the result. The derivative is the covariant derivative $D\psi = d\psi + \omega \mathsf T \psi$ of Part~\ref{part:ga}, with the connection form $\omega$ of the face. If the transport of step 3 makes the face flat, $\omega = 0$ inside it and $D$ is the ordinary gradient; that is the case in Parts~\ref{part:matrix} to~\ref{part:intrinsic}, and also, by decision rather than necessity, in Part~\ref{part:dec}. Part~\ref{part:edge} gives the face the curved connection~(\ref{eqn:symmetric_gauge}).

\paragraph{Step 6: the element matrix.} The integral of $|D\psi|^2$ over a face, written as a Hermitian $3 \times 3$ matrix in the three vertex values. For a flat face it is always the scalar cotangent element with a phase on every entry: $\mathrm L^f_{ab} = A_f\, \nabla\phi_a \cdot \nabla\phi_b\; e^{\mathsf T \delta_{ab}}$, where $e^{\mathsf T\delta_{ab}}$ is the transport from $b$ to $a$ that the method uses \emph{in that face}. The curved element of Part~\ref{part:edge} changes the integrand itself.

\paragraph{Steps 7 and 8: assembly, solve, draw.} The same in every part. The element matrices are added into an $n \times n$ Hermitian matrix $\mathrm L$; the lumped mass is $\mathrm M_{ii} = \frac13 \sum_{f \ni i} A_f$; the backward Euler step $(\mathrm M + \Delta t\, \mathrm L)\psi = \mathrm M \psi^0$ is scaled by $\mathrm M^{-1}$, the row of the source is replaced by the prescribed value, and the system is solved by sparse LU (Section~\ref{section:solve}). Finally $\psi_i / |\psi_i|$ is drawn, which needs the positions again (the function \code{lift}, Section~\ref{section:int_fem}).

\paragraph{Why so few steps differ.} The weak form, the Galerkin method, linear hat functions, assembly and the solver are standard finite element machinery, the same as for the scalar heat equation \cite{Sayas2015, Langtangen2014}. The only ingredient that the scalar method does not have is the comparison of vectors at different points, the connection, and every difference between the methods is a different way of discretizing it.

%%% ----------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Three Choices, and One That Is Not a Choice}
\label{section:map_choices}

\paragraph{Summary.} The methods are the cells of a small grid (Figure~\ref{fig:choices}): choice A, the vertex planes (extrinsic or intrinsic), times choice B, the transport (corner or edge), with choice C, the element, nearly forced by B. The notation (matrices, geometric algebra, complex numbers) is not a fourth choice: it changes no number.

\begin{figure}[t]
\centering
\adjustbox{max width=\linewidth}{\input{figures/fig_choices}}
\caption{The methods of this text as cells of a grid. Rows: choice A, how a vertex gets a plane. Columns: choice B, what the transport connects. Inside each cell, choice C, the element, and the consequences: where the curvature lives, and whether a mesh that is bent but intrinsically flat is treated exactly. The empty cell is discussed in Section~\ref{section:map_choices}.}
\label{fig:choices}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Choice A: how a vertex gets a plane}

\emph{Extrinsic} (Parts~\ref{part:matrix}, \ref{part:ga}): the plane orthogonal to the vertex normal, with each face rolled into it by the smallest rotation of space. It uses one piece of information more than the lengths, the tilt of the faces. That information helps when the mesh samples a smooth surface whose normals it approximates well (the coarse spheres of Section~\ref{section:int_experiments}, where Part~\ref{part:ga} is the most accurate), and it hurts when the tilt is not related to the intrinsic geometry, as on the Miura-ori, where it produces errors of up to $21.9^\circ$ on a mesh that is isometric to a flat sheet. The construction fails when a face normal is opposite to the vertex normal.

\emph{Intrinsic} (Parts~\ref{part:intrinsic} to~\ref{part:dec}): the cone of directions stretched by $s_i = 2\pi/\Theta_i$. It uses only the lengths, so it gives the same answer on all isometric meshes: exactly the answer of the flat sheet on every bent flat mesh. It never fails for non-degenerate triangles.

\subsection{Choice B: what the transport connects}

\emph{Corner transport} (Parts~\ref{part:matrix} to~\ref{part:intrinsic}): an angle $\rho_i^f$ for every pair of a vertex and a face around it. A vertex value is carried into a face; two vertex values meet inside the face. \emph{Edge transport} (Parts~\ref{part:edge}, \ref{part:dec}): an angle $\rho_{ab}$ for every oriented edge, with $\rho_{ba} = -\rho_{ab}$ (up to whole turns). A vertex value is carried directly into the plane of a neighbor. Section~\ref{section:map_transport} shows that the two differ by a small, explicit correction, and Section~\ref{section:map_form} that only the second is a discrete 1-form of the mesh.

\subsection{Choice C: the element, nearly forced by B}

With corner transport, the three values of a face are carried into one flat frame of that face, and around the face the transports compose to the identity: $(\rho_a - \rho_b) + (\rho_b - \rho_c) + (\rho_c - \rho_a) = 0$. There is nothing to curve, and the element is the flat one~(\ref{eqn:intrinsic_element}). With edge transport, the three transports around a face compose to $e^{\mathsf T\Omega_f}$, which is not the identity, and no flat frame of the face can agree with all three of them (Section~\ref{section:edge_element}). Then there are exactly three things one can do:
\begin{enumerate}
\item give the face a connection with total curvature $\Omega_f$, and integrate the energy of the curved trial functions: the curved element of Part~\ref{part:edge}, conforming, with quadrature;
\item keep the flat element but put the edge transport of each pair on its entry anyway: DEC, Part~\ref{part:dec}, a variational crime of order $\Omega_f$;
\item choose for each corner the angle $\sigma_a^f$ that makes a flat frame agree with the three edges as well as possible. But a choice of one angle per corner \emph{is} a corner transport, and its own transports around the face close; the mismatch with the edge transports puts the curvature back at the vertices. This option is corner transport again, and nothing new.
\end{enumerate}
So the element is not an independent choice: B decides it, except for the decision between options 1 and 2.

\subsection{The empty cell}

Extrinsic planes with edge transport were not built in this text. They are easy to define: keep the angle of a vector to the edge, as in~(\ref{eqn:edge_transport}), but measure the angle of the edge in each vertex plane after projecting the edge into that plane. The face holonomies would then add up to $2\pi\chi$ as long as the projected edges keep their order around each vertex. On a bent flat mesh, however, the projected angles differ from the true ones, so the faces would get holonomies that the paper does not have, and the method would not be exact on the Miura-ori. The extrinsic error moves from the vertices into the faces; it does not disappear. Since the purpose of Part~\ref{part:edge} was a method that is exact on bent flat meshes, this cell was left empty.

\subsection{The notation is not a choice}

Part~\ref{part:matrix} writes every transport as a real $2 \times 2$ rotation, Part~\ref{part:ga} as a unit complex number of the face plane $e^{\mathsf T\rho}$ computed with rotors of space, and Parts~\ref{part:intrinsic} to~\ref{part:dec} as a \code{std::complex}. On the cactus the real and the complex systems agree to $6 \cdot 10^{-16}$ (Section~\ref{section:ga_code}). The notation decides how clearly the method can be explained and how much code is needed, not which numbers come out. Section~\ref{section:map_notation} says what each notation is good for.

%%% ----------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Where the Curvature Lives}
\label{section:map_curvature}

\paragraph{Summary.} Every method keeps the whole curvature of the mesh, $2\pi\chi$; they differ in where they put it. Parts~\ref{part:matrix} and~\ref{part:ga} put it at the vertices, in the gaps between the rolled corners, unevenly, according to the tilt of the faces. Part~\ref{part:intrinsic} puts it at the vertices, in every corner in proportion to its angle. Part~\ref{part:edge} puts the same shares inside the faces, spread evenly. Part~\ref{part:dec} keeps the same shares on the boundaries of the faces, and treats the inside as flat (Figure~\ref{fig:curvature_moves}).

\begin{figure}[t]
\centering
\adjustbox{max width=\linewidth}{\input{figures/fig_curvature_moves}}
\caption{The leaning pyramid of Example~\ref{ex:int_leaning}, seen from its apex, with an angle defect of $69.5^\circ$; corners counterclockwise from the reference edge (blue). Each method keeps the $69.5^\circ$ somewhere else (red numbers, adding up to $69.5^\circ$ in every panel). II: in the gaps between the corners rolled into the plane of the vertex normal. III: in every corner, stretched by $s_i = 1.2391$. IV: the same shares, as the holonomies of the five faces, spread evenly inside each face (the shade is the density $\kappa = \Omega_f/A_f$). V: the same holonomies, kept only in the transports around each face.}
\label{fig:curvature_moves}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{The upright pyramid.} The apex of the pyramid over a regular pentagon with equilateral faces (Example~\ref{ex:int_pyramid}) has five corners of $60^\circ$, $\Theta = 300^\circ$, a defect of $60^\circ$ and $s = 1.2$. Part~\ref{part:ga} rolls the five faces into the horizontal plane and leaves five gaps of $12^\circ$. Part~\ref{part:intrinsic} stretches each corner to $72^\circ$: each absorbs $12^\circ$. Part~\ref{part:edge} computes the edge transports~(\ref{eqn:edge_transport}), and each face gets the holonomy $\Omega_f = (s - 1)\cdot 60^\circ = 12^\circ$~(\ref{eqn:face_holonomy}). The base vertices are on the boundary ($s = 1$) and contribute nothing. Five times $12^\circ$ is $60^\circ$ in all four methods, and by symmetry it is distributed in the same way; the methods agree here, and the program finds the four solutions with the source at the apex equal to rounding at every base corner.

\paragraph{The leaning pyramid.} Moving the apex by $0.4$ breaks the symmetry (Example~\ref{ex:int_leaning}): the defect is $69.47^\circ$, $s = 1.2391$, and the corners are $70.49^\circ$, $70.49^\circ$, $51.37^\circ$, $46.81^\circ$, $51.37^\circ$. Part~\ref{part:intrinsic} gives each corner $(s - 1)\alpha$: $16.85^\circ$, $16.85^\circ$, $12.28^\circ$, $11.19^\circ$, $12.28^\circ$. Part~\ref{part:edge} gives each face the holonomy $\Omega_f = \sum_v (s_v - 1)\alpha_v$, and since the base vertices have $s = 1$, these are the same five numbers, now attached to the faces. Part~\ref{part:ga} leaves the gaps $23.66^\circ$, $15.18^\circ$, $7.72^\circ$, $7.72^\circ$, $15.18^\circ$, which depend on how far each face is tilted from the vertex normal. With the source at the apex, the solutions of Parts~\ref{part:intrinsic} and~\ref{part:edge} differ at the base corners by $0.09^\circ$ at most, DEC differs from Part~\ref{part:intrinsic} by $0.97^\circ$, and Part~\ref{part:ga} by $10.9^\circ$.

\paragraph{The sum is fixed, the place is not.} In all four methods the total over a closed mesh is $2\pi\chi$. For Parts~\ref{part:intrinsic} to~\ref{part:dec} this is the discrete Gauss--Bonnet theorem, because $\sum_f \Omega_f = \sum_v (s_v - 1)\Theta_v = \sum_v \kappa_v$ (Section~\ref{section:edge_transport}). For Part~\ref{part:ga} the gaps around each vertex add up to its defect (Section~\ref{section:int_vertex}). So the methods do not disagree about \emph{how much} curvature the mesh has; they disagree about \emph{where} inside a one-ring it is. On a fine mesh of a smooth surface the defect of every vertex is small, $s_i \to 1$, all the shares shrink, and the place stops mattering: that is why all four converge to the same answer (Figure~\ref{fig:four_plots}). On a coarse, strongly curved mesh the place is a modeling decision, worth a few degrees on the cactus (Section~\ref{section:dec_results}).

\paragraph{Consequences of the place.} Three facts of the earlier parts follow from this section alone.
\begin{itemize}
\item \emph{Exactness on bent flat meshes.} If every vertex is flat, $\Theta_i = 2\pi$, the intrinsic shares are all zero and there is no curvature anywhere for Parts~\ref{part:intrinsic} to~\ref{part:dec}: they are exact. The gaps of Part~\ref{part:ga} cancel in total at such a vertex but not one by one, unless all the faces are rolled about one line, which is why only the parallel folds are exact for it.
\item \emph{The accuracy of DEC on coarse meshes.} DEC knows $\Omega_f$ only through the transports on the boundary of the face; the trial functions inside are flat. The terms it drops are of order $\Omega_f$, so it is the least accurate method when the faces hold large holonomies, the coarse spheres, and the difference disappears when they do not (Section~\ref{section:dec_results}).
\item \emph{The cost of a curved face.} Spreading $\Omega_f$ inside the face, Part~\ref{part:edge}, makes the element conforming but requires quadrature, and on the vector heat method it buys nothing over Part~\ref{part:intrinsic}. On the energy of a smooth field it buys a factor of seven to ten in accuracy (Section~\ref{section:map_forms}).
\end{itemize}

%%% ----------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Corner Transport and Edge Transport, Exactly}
\label{section:map_transport}

\paragraph{Summary.} The corner transport of Part~\ref{part:intrinsic}, followed from a vertex into a face and out to another vertex, is the edge transport of Part~\ref{part:edge} along the same edge, turned by half the stretching of the two corners at the ends of the edge in that face (Proposition~\ref{prop:corner_edge}). The two faces of an edge turn it in opposite directions, so the corner transport along an edge depends on the face it goes through, and the difference between the two faces, $H_{ij}$, is where Part~\ref{part:intrinsic} keeps its curvature.

\begin{figure}[t]
\centering
\adjustbox{max width=\linewidth}{\input{figures/fig_corner_edge}}
\caption{Left: corner transport connects every vertex with every face around it (green). Around one face it closes, but going from $i$ to $j$ through $f$ and coming back through $g$ turns a vector by $H_{ij}$, (\ref{eqn:edge_loop}). Right: edge transport connects neighboring vertices directly (orange), with one transport per edge that both faces share; around a face it turns by $\Omega_f$.}
\label{fig:corner_edge}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Two vertices compared through a face}

In the face $f$, the value $\psi_b$ of vertex $b$ is carried into the face by $e^{\mathsf T\rho_b^f}$ and from there into the plane of vertex $a$ by $e^{-\mathsf T\rho_a^f}$. So corner transport defines, for every face and every pair of its vertices, a vertex-to-vertex transport
\begin{equation}
\label{eqn:corner_vertex}
\text{from } b \text{ to } a \text{ through } f: \quad e^{\mathsf T(\rho_b^f - \rho_a^f)} ,
\end{equation}
the phase that the element~(\ref{eqn:intrinsic_element}) puts on its entry $(a, b)$. An interior edge $ab$ has two faces, so it gets two transports, one through each, and nothing makes them equal. Edge transport instead fixes one transport per edge, $e^{\mathsf T\rho_{ab}}$, which both faces use. How far apart are the two? The answer is short.

\begin{prop}
\label{prop:corner_edge}
Let $f$ be the face to the left of the half edge from $a$ to $b$, with corner angles $\alpha_a^f$ at $a$ and $\alpha_b^f$ at $b$, and let $s_a, s_b$ be the scales~(\ref{eqn:scale}) of the two vertices. Then the corner transport of Part~\ref{part:intrinsic} from $a$ to $b$ through $f$ and the edge transport of Part~\ref{part:edge} are related by
\begin{equation}
\label{eqn:corner_edge}
\rho_a^f - \rho_b^f = \rho_{ab} - \tfrac12 \big[ (s_a - 1)\, \alpha_a^f + (s_b - 1)\, \alpha_b^f \big] .
\end{equation}
Through the face $g$ to the right of the half edge the sign of the correction is reversed: $\rho_a^g - \rho_b^g = \rho_{ab} + \tfrac12 \big[ (s_a - 1)\, \alpha_a^g + (s_b - 1)\, \alpha_b^g \big]$.
\end{prop}
\begin{proof}
Let $c$ be the third vertex of $f$, so that $a, b, c$ are counterclockwise. Measure angles at each vertex with the cone angles $\theta$ of~(\ref{eqn:cone_angle}), and write $\theta_{ab}$ for the cone angle at $a$ of the half edge from $a$ to $b$. The corner of $f$ at $a$ begins at the edge to $b$: in the cone of $a$ it is the interval $[\theta_{ab}, \theta_{ab} + \alpha_a^f]$. The corner of $f$ at $b$ begins at the edge to $c$ and ends at the edge to $a$: it is $[\theta_{bc}, \theta_{bc} + \alpha_b^f]$, with $\theta_{ba} = \theta_{bc} + \alpha_b^f$. In the face frame let $\beta_{ab}$ and $\beta_{bc}$ be the directions of the edges from $a$ to $b$ and from $b$ to $c$; walking counterclockwise around the face, the direction turns left by $\pi - \alpha_b^f$ at $b$, so $\beta_{bc} = \beta_{ab} + \pi - \alpha_b^f$. By~(\ref{eqn:intrinsic_rho}),
\begin{equation*}
\rho_a^f = \beta_{ab} + \tfrac12\alpha_a^f - s_a\big(\theta_{ab} + \tfrac12\alpha_a^f\big), \qquad
\rho_b^f = \beta_{bc} + \tfrac12\alpha_b^f - s_b\big(\theta_{bc} + \tfrac12\alpha_b^f\big),
\end{equation*}
and by~(\ref{eqn:edge_transport}), with $\varphi = s\theta$, $\rho_{ab} = s_b\theta_{ba} + \pi - s_a\theta_{ab}$. Subtract, and replace $\beta_{bc}$ and $\theta_{ba}$:
\begin{align*}
(\rho_a^f - \rho_b^f) - \rho_{ab}
&= \beta_{ab} - \beta_{bc} - \pi + \tfrac12 (1 - s_a)\alpha_a^f - \tfrac12\alpha_b^f + s_b\theta_{bc} + \tfrac12 s_b \alpha_b^f - s_b \theta_{ba} \\
&= (\alpha_b^f - 2\pi) + \tfrac12 (1 - s_a)\alpha_a^f - \tfrac12\alpha_b^f - s_b \alpha_b^f + \tfrac12 s_b\alpha_b^f \\
&= -\tfrac12 (s_a - 1)\alpha_a^f - \tfrac12 (s_b - 1)\alpha_b^f - 2\pi ,
\end{align*}
which is~(\ref{eqn:corner_edge}) up to a whole turn. For $g$, apply this to the half edge from $b$ to $a$, which has $g$ to its left, and use $\rho_{ba} \equiv -\rho_{ab}$.
\end{proof}

The proposition is checked by \code{ga\_fem}: on every half edge of the cactus the two sides of~(\ref{eqn:corner_edge}) agree to $1.4 \cdot 10^{-13}$ degrees, while the corrections themselves reach $13.5^\circ$. The check divides the corner transport through the face by the edge transport of the same half edge, and compares the angle of the quotient with the prediction:
\lstinputlisting[firstline=849, lastline=863, firstnumber=849]{../src/ga_fem.cpp} On the upright pyramid every correction along an edge from the apex is $\mp 6^\circ$, half of the stretching $(1.2 - 1)\cdot 60^\circ$ of the apex corner (the base corners have $s = 1$).

\paragraph{Reading the proposition.} In words: carrying a vector from $a$ into $f$ and out to $b$, Part~\ref{part:intrinsic} keeps its angle to the edge $ab$, as the edge transport does, except that each corner is stretched, and the edge sits at the side of the stretched corner, displaced by half the stretching (Figure~\ref{fig:corner_map}). So:
\begin{enumerate}
\item On a flat vertex, $s = 1$, there is no correction: corner transport and edge transport are the same rule, the unfolding.
\item Summed around the face $f$, every corner appears twice, at the start of one edge and at the end of another, and the corrections add up to $-\sum_v (s_v - 1)\alpha_v^f = -\Omega_f$: exactly what is needed to cancel the holonomy of the edge transport, so that corner transport closes around every face, as it must.
\item The element~(\ref{eqn:intrinsic_element}) of Part~\ref{part:intrinsic} and the DEC element of Part~\ref{part:dec} have the same moduli, and their phases differ on every entry by the correction~(\ref{eqn:corner_edge}). DEC is Part~\ref{part:intrinsic} with the corrections removed; Part~\ref{part:edge} removes them too, and puts the curvature into the trial functions instead.
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{The edge loop: where Part III keeps its curvature}

Go from $a$ to $b$ through $f$ and come back from $b$ to $a$ through $g$ (Figure~\ref{fig:corner_edge}, left). By the proposition, the vector comes back turned by
\begin{equation}
\label{eqn:edge_loop}
H_{ab} = \tfrac12 \big[ (s_a - 1)(\alpha_a^f + \alpha_a^g) + (s_b - 1)(\alpha_b^f + \alpha_b^g) \big] ,
\end{equation}
the transport through $g$ minus the transport through $f$. This is the curvature of corner transport: it is not on the faces, where corner transport closes, but on the loops that cross an edge from one face to the other. Each vertex contributes, to each of its edges, half the stretching of the two corners on either side of that edge. Summing over the edges of a closed mesh, every corner is counted once for each of its two sides, so
\begin{equation}
\label{eqn:edge_loop_sum}
\sum_{\text{edges}} H_{ab} = \sum_v (s_v - 1)\, \Theta_v = \sum_v \kappa_v = 2\pi\chi ,
\end{equation}
which the program finds on the cactus, $4\pi$ to all digits. On the leaning pyramid the five edges from the apex have $H = 16.85^\circ$, $14.57^\circ$, $11.74^\circ$, $11.74^\circ$, $14.57^\circ$ (each the mean of the shares of its two faces), adding up to the defect $69.47^\circ$. The same loop with the corner transport of Part~\ref{part:ga} turns by $23.66^\circ$, $15.18^\circ$, $7.72^\circ$, $7.72^\circ$, $15.18^\circ$: exactly the gaps between the rolled corners on either side of each edge.
